\{\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}\}
, the number of configurations
\sigma
such that
\omega\sim_{\mathfrak{g}}\sigma
is
2^{\tilde{k}(\omega)-1}
, where we emphasise that
\tilde{k}(\omega)
is the number of components in
\omega
after identifying
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{-}
. Conversely, if
\omega\sim_{\mathfrak{g}}\sigma
, then
\omega\in\{\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}\}
. Therefore, we can write

{\bf Z}^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta,\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,M},\mathsf{a}}[\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}]\\
=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\in\{0,1\}^{E(\mathcal{R}^{+,-}(L,M))}\\
\mathfrak{g}^{+}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.20757pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.20757pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.8375pt\kern-3.04367pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.3125pt\kern-2.49922pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{-}\end{subarray}}2^{\tilde{k}(\omega)-1}\prod_{xy\in E(\mathcal{R}(L,M))}\Big(\frac{p(\beta,\mathsf{a})_{xy}}{1-p(\beta,\mathsf{a})_{xy}}\Big)^{\omega_{xy}}\prod_{x\in\mathcal{R}(L,M)}\Big(\frac{p(\beta,\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,M},\mathsf{a})_{x\mathfrak{g}}}{1-p(\beta,\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,M},\mathsf{a})_{x\mathfrak{g}}}\Big)^{\omega_{x\mathfrak{g}}}\\
=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{R}^{+,-}(L,M)}\\
\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}^{+}}=1\\
\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}^{-}}=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\omega\sim_{\mathfrak{g}}\sigma}\prod_{xy\in E(\mathcal{R}(L,M))}\Big(\frac{p(\beta,\mathsf{a})_{xy}}{1-p(\beta,\mathsf{a})_{xy}}\Big)^{\omega_{xy}}\prod_{x\in\mathcal{R}(L,M)}\Big(\frac{p(\beta,\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,M},\mathsf{a})_{x\mathfrak{g}}}{1-p(\beta,\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,M},\mathsf{a})_{x\mathfrak{g}}}\Big)^{\omega_{x\mathfrak{g}}}.

(5.11)

From there, the end of the computation is exactly the same as in the proof of (
4.9
).
∎

5.2
Strict positivity of the surface tension

Our aim now is to show that the strict inequality
\tau_{\beta}>0
holds in the entire supercritical regime— see Proposition
5.5
. This follows from an adaptation of the argument of
[
LP81
]
, which crucially relies on the Ginibre inequality (Proposition
2.4
).

We will need the following lemma.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\beta>0
and
L\geq 1
. Then, both
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}
converge weakly as
M\to\infty
. We write

\langle\cdot\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}:=\lim_{M\to\infty}\langle\cdot\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}\quad\text{and}\quad\langle\cdot\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}:=\lim_{M\to\infty}\langle\cdot\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}.

(5.12)

Proof.

Observe that by Proposition
2.2
, one has that
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}^{d},\beta,1}\succcurlyeq\langle\cdot\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}
for every
L,M\geq 1
. Moreover, the measure
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}^{d},\beta,1}
can be obtained as the limit of the sequence
(\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta,1})_{n\geq 1}
, which is uniformly regular in the sense of Proposition
2.6
, and hence
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}^{d},\beta,1}
inherits this property. In particular, this implies that the measures
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}
are also uniformly regular, and therefore tight. Using again Proposition
2.2
, the sequence
(\langle\cdot\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta})_{M\geq 1}
is stochastically increasing and hence converges as
M\rightarrow\infty
.
For
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}
, note that by flipping the sign of all spins
\varphi_{x}
for
x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}\times\{-M,\ldots,0\}
, and denoting the resulting configuration
\tilde{\varphi}
, we obtain

\langle F(\varphi)\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}=\frac{\langle F(\tilde{\varphi})\prod_{x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}}\exp(-2\beta\varphi_{(x,1)}\varphi_{(x,0)})\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}}{\langle\prod_{x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}}\exp(-2\beta\varphi_{(x,1)}\varphi_{(x,0)})\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}}

(5.13)

for every bounded (local) measurable function
F
. The convergence of
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}
then follows from the convergence and regularity of
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}
.
∎

Proposition 5.5
.

For every
\beta>\beta_{c}
,

\tau_{\beta}\geq\frac{1}{d}\int_{\beta_{c}}^{\beta}\left(\langle\varphi_{0}\rangle_{u}^{+}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}u.

(5.14)

In particular,
\tau_{\beta}>0
.

Proof.

Our main tool will be the Ginibre inequality stated in Proposition
2.4
.
We first differentiate
\tau^{L,M}_{\beta}
with respect to
\beta
to get that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\rm d}{\rm d\beta}\tau^{L,M}_{\beta}=\frac{1}{L^{d-1}}&\sum_{xy\in E(\mathcal{R}(L,M))}\left(\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}-\langle\varphi_{x}\varphi_{y}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}\right)\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{L^{d-1}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{R}(L,M)}\left(\langle\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,M}(x)\varphi_{x}\rangle_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}-\langle\mathsf{h}^{+,-}_{L,M}(x)\varphi_{x}\rangle_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}\right).\end{split}

(5.15)

By (
2.9
), each term in the sums is non-negative. Let us fix some
N\geq 1
. By disregarding the horizontal edges and the edges in the complement of
E(\mathcal{R}(L,N))
, and applying Proposition
2.4
, we obtain, for every
M\geq N
,

\displaystyle\frac{\rm d}{\rm d\beta}\tau^{L,M}_{\beta}\geq\frac{1}{L^{d-1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}\\
j\in\{-N,\ldots,N-1\}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\Big(\langle\varphi_{(x,j)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}\langle\varphi_{(x,j+1)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}

\displaystyle\qquad-\langle\varphi_{(x,j+1)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}\langle\varphi_{(x,j)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}\Big).

Taking the limit as
M\to\infty
, and using translation invariance in the
\mathbf{e}_{d}
direction we obtain

\displaystyle\liminf_{M\to\infty}\frac{\rm d}{\rm d\beta}\tau^{L,M}_{\beta}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{L^{d-1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}\\
j\in\{-N,\ldots,N-1\}\end{subarray}}\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\left(\langle\varphi_{(x,j+1)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}-\langle\varphi_{(x,j)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L^{d-1}}\sum_{x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}}\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\left(\langle\varphi_{(x,N)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}-\langle\varphi_{(x,-N)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\right).

Now, we can use Lemma
5.6
below to obtain that

\liminf_{M\to\infty}\frac{\rm d}{\rm d\beta}\tau^{L,M}_{\beta}\geq\frac{1}{L^{d-1}}\sum_{x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}}2\left(\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\right)^{2}.

(5.16)

Applying Fatou’s lemma we can now deduce that

\tau_{\beta}^{L}\geq\tau_{\beta_{c}}^{L}+\frac{1}{L^{d-1}}\sum_{x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}}2\int_{\beta_{c}}^{\beta}\left(\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L),u}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}u.

(5.17)

Without loss of generality, we only handle
\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L),u}
for
x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}
satisfying
x_{1}=|x|\in\{0,\ldots,L\}
. Using Proposition
2.3
, for
u\in(\beta_{c},\beta)
and
x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}
satisfying
x_{1}=|x|\in\{0,\ldots,L\}
, one has

\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L),u}\geq\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L,L-x_{1}),u}\geq\langle\varphi_{0}\rangle_{\Lambda_{L-x_{1}}(x),u,\tilde{\mathsf{h}}_{L-x_{1}}(x)},

(5.18)

where
\tilde{\mathsf{h}}_{L-x_{1}}(x)
was defined above Proposition
3.3
. The latter proposition gives that

\liminf_{L\rightarrow\infty}\langle\varphi_{0}\rangle_{\Lambda_{L-x_{1}}(x),u,\tilde{\mathsf{h}}_{L-x_{1}}(x)}\geq\frac{1}{2d}\langle\varphi_{0}\rangle^{+}_{u}.

(5.19)

It follows that

\tau_{\beta}\geq\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{1}{dL^{d-1}}\sum_{x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}}\int_{\beta_{c}}^{\beta}\left(\langle\varphi_{0}\rangle^{+}_{u}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}u=\frac{1}{d}\int_{\beta_{c}}^{\beta}\left(\langle\varphi_{0}\rangle^{+}_{u}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}u>0,

(5.20)

as desired.
∎

We now prove the lemma mentioned in the proof of Proposition
5.5
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\beta>0
. For every
L\geq 1
we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\langle\varphi_{(x,N)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}=\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{N\to\infty}\langle\varphi_{(x,-N)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}=-\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}

(5.21)

for every
x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}
.

Proof.

We will only obtain the limit of
\langle\varphi_{(x,N)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}
. The limit of
\langle\varphi_{(x,-N)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}
can be obtained similarly, once one observes that
-\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}=\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{-,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}
.

Let us define

\iota:=\min\{i>0:~\varphi_{(x,2i)}>0,\,\forall x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}\}.

(5.22)

We will first prove that
\iota
is finite almost surely.

Reasoning as in the proof of Lemma
5.4
, the stochastic domination (by a product measure) of Proposition
2.6
extends to the measure
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}
. Therefore, there exists
M>0
such that
\langle\cdot\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}
–almost surely, there are infinitely many
i>0
such that the event

A_{M,i}:=\{|\varphi_{(x,\ell)}|\leq M,\>\forall x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1},\forall\ell\in\{2i-1,2i+1\}\}

(5.23)

occurs. Let
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\rm odd}(L):=\{(x,2j+1):x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1},\>j\geq 0\}
. Using the domain Markov property, we obtain that

\nu^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\left[\Big\{\varphi_{(x,2i)}>0,\,\forall x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}\Big\}\mid\varphi_{|\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\rm odd}(L)}=\eta_{|\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\rm odd}(L)}\right],

(5.24)

remains bounded away from
0
, uniformly over
i
and
\eta_{|\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\rm odd}(L)}\in A_{M,i}
. Furthermore, these events are independent of each other conditionally on
\varphi_{|\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\rm odd}(L)}
. Therefore,
\iota
is indeed finite almost surely.

Let us now condition on the event
\{\iota=i\}
, which is measurable with respect to
\varphi_{(x,j)}
,
j\leq 2i
and
x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}
. By the Markov property and monotonicity in boundary condition, we have that for every
N\geq 2i

\langle\varphi_{(x,N)}\mid\iota=i\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\geq\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L,N-2i),\beta}.

(5.25)

As a consequence,

\langle\varphi_{(x,N)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\geq\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L,N-2i),\beta}\langle\mathds{1}_{\iota\leq i}\rangle_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}^{+,-}+\langle\varphi_{(x,N)}\mathds{1}_{\iota>i}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}.

(5.26)

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and Proposition
2.6
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for every
i\geq 1
we have

|\langle\varphi_{(x,N)}\mathbbm{1}_{\iota>i}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}|\leq\sqrt{\langle\varphi^{2}_{(x,N)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\langle\mathbbm{1}_{\iota>i}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}}\leq C\sqrt{\langle\mathbbm{1}_{\iota>i}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}}=:\varepsilon_{i}.

(5.27)

Thus, by Lemma
5.4
, for every
i>0
,

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\langle\varphi_{(x,N)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\geq\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\langle\mathbbm{1}_{\iota\leq i}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}-\varepsilon_{i}.

(5.28)

Since
\iota
is finite almost surely,
\langle\mathbbm{1}_{\iota\leq i}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\rightarrow 1
and
\varepsilon_{i}\rightarrow 0
as
i\rightarrow\infty
. We can deduce that

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\langle\varphi_{(x,N)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\geq\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}.

(5.29)

Moreover, by Proposition
2.4
(with
A=\{(x,N)\}
and
B=\emptyset
), for every
N
,

\langle\varphi_{(x,N)}\rangle^{+,-}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}\leq\langle\varphi_{(x,0)}\rangle^{+,+}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta}.

(5.30)

The proof follows readily from the two last displayed equations.

∎

5.3
Comparison with the free
\varphi^{4}
random cluster model

In this section we prove Theorem
5.1
. Proposition
5.5
and Lemma
5.3
readily imply a surface order bound on disconnection for the
\varphi^{4}
random cluster measure
\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta,\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L}}:=\lim_{M\to\infty}\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta,\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,M}}
— see Lemma
5.7
. Theorem
5.1
then follows from a comparison to the free measure
\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\beta}
(for
\delta>0
well chosen). This comparison is established in two steps in Lemmas
5.8
and
5.9
. The uniqueness of the
\varphi^{4}
random cluster measure on the half-space with positive magnetic field (recall Propositions
3.6
and
4.15
) will be crucial in the later step.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\beta>\beta_{c}
. There exists
c_{2}>0
such that for every
L\geq 1
,

\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta,\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L}}[\mathfrak{g}^{-}\longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{+}]\geq 1-e^{-c_{2}L^{d-1}}.

(5.31)

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
5.3
and Proposition
5.5
that

\limsup_{L\to\infty}\frac{1}{L^{d-1}}\log\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta,\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L}}[\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}]<0.

(5.32)

Since
\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta,\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L}}[\mathfrak{g}^{-}\longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{+}]>0
for every
L\geq 1
, there exists a constant
c_{2}>0
such that

\frac{1}{L^{d-1}}\log\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\beta,\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L}}[\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}]\leq-c_{2}

(5.33)

for every
L\geq 1
. This concludes the proof.
∎

We now obtain a finite volume analogue of Lemma
5.7
for a
\varphi^{4}
random cluster measure defined on a rectangle with sufficiently large external magnetic field on the top and bottom faces. More precisely, for
h>0
, let
\Psi^{0,h}_{\mathcal{R}(L,M),\beta}
denote the
\varphi^{4}
random cluster measure on
\mathcal{R}(L,M)
with external magnetic field equal to
h
on
\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,M)\cup\partial^{\rm top}\mathcal{R}(L,M)
and
0
otherwise, where

\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,M):=\{x\in\mathcal{R}(L,M):x_{d}=-M\},\;\partial^{\rm top}\mathcal{R}(L,M):=\{x\in\mathcal{R}(L,M):x_{d}=M\}.

(5.34)

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\beta>\beta_{c}
. For every
h>0
sufficiently large, there exist
\delta>0
and
c_{3}=c_{3}(h)>0
such that for every
L\geq 1
,

\Psi^{0,h}_{R(L,\delta L),\beta}[\partial^{{\rm bot}}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)\longleftrightarrow\partial^{{\rm top}}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)]\geq 1-e^{-c_{3}L^{d-1}}.

(5.35)

Proof.

We fix
\beta>\beta_{c}
and drop it from notation. Let
\delta>0
be a constant to be determined. Recall Lemma
5.7
. Our aim is to first compare
\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L}}
to
\Psi^{0,h}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)}
up to a cost that we can make smaller than
e^{(c_{2}/2)L^{d-1}}
by tuning the value of
h
, and then forcing a particular set of edges to be closed, which in turn forces any path from
\mathfrak{g}^{-}
to
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
to connect
\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)
to
\partial^{\rm top}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)
. To achieve the latter at a small cost we need to choose
\delta
sufficiently small. This naturally splits the proof into two steps.

Step 1.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be a constant to be determined. Introduce
A:=A^{\rm top}\cup A^{\rm bot}
, where
A^{\rm top}=\partial^{\rm top}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L+1)
and
A^{\rm bot}=\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L+1)
. By the FKG inequality and regularity, we can now choose
H>0
to be large enough so that

\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L}}[\mathsf{a}|_{A}\leq H]\geq e^{-c_{2}/2L^{d-1}},

(5.36)

where
c_{2}
is the constant of Lemma
5.7
. Thus,

\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L}}[\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}]\geq\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L}}[\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}\mid\mathsf{a}|_{A}\leq H]e^{-c_{2}/2L^{d-1}}.

(5.37)

By Proposition
4.10
, and the FKG inequality of Proposition
4.9
(for the conditional measure
\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L}}[\>\cdot\mid\mathsf{a}|_{A}=H]
),

\displaystyle\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L}}[\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}\mid\mathsf{a}|_{A}\leq H]

\displaystyle\geq\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L}}[\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}\mid\mathsf{a}|_{A}=H]

(5.38)

\displaystyle\geq\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L}}[\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}\mid\mathsf{a}|_{A}=H,\mathcal{A}],

where

\mathcal{A}:=\{\omega_{e}=1,\>\forall e\in E(A)\}\cap\{\exists\,(x,y)\in A^{\rm top}\times A^{\rm bot}:\>\omega_{x\mathfrak{g}^{+}}=1,\>\omega_{y\mathfrak{g}^{-}}=1\}.

(5.39)

Let us denote
\mathsf{H}
the external magnetic field which is equal to
H
on
\partial^{\rm top}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)\cup\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)
. Since on the event
\mathcal{A}
,
A^{\rm top}
is connected to
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
, and
A^{\rm bot}
is connected to
\mathfrak{g}^{-}
, the domain Markov property of Proposition
4.4
gives

\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L}}[\>\cdot\mid\mathsf{a}|_{A}=H,\mathcal{A}]=\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}}.

(5.40)

Combining (
5.37
) and (
5.40
), and applying Lemma
5.7
, we obtain

\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}}[\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}]\leq e^{-c_{2}/2L^{d-1}}.

(5.41)

[formula]
[formula]

Figure 6:
An illustration of (
5.44
). The magnetic field
\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}
is supported on the coloured regions: in the red region it is equal to
1
, in the green region it is equal to
H
, and in the overlap of the two regions— in orange— it is equal to
H+1
. This provides three different ways to connect to either of the two ghost vertices. If there exists an open path in
\omega
connecting
\mathfrak{g}^{+}
to
\mathfrak{g}^{-}
and additionally the graph
T
— depicted by the dotted red line— is fully closed in
\omega
, then there must exist an open path from
\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)
to
\partial^{\rm top}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)
.

Step 2.

We now move to the second part of the proof.
We begin by defining the graph
T=(V(T),E(T))
defined as follows:

V(T):=\Big\{x\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}\times\{0,1\}:L-\log L\leq|x|\leq L\Big\}\\
\cup\Big\{x\in\partial\Lambda_{L-\log L}\cup\partial\Lambda_{L-\log L+1}:|x_{d}|\leq\delta L\Big\},

(5.42)

and
E(T):=\{xy\in E(\mathbb{Z}^{d}):x,y\in V(T)\}
. Let us define the event

\mathcal{E}:=\{\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\partial^{\rm top}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)\}.

(5.43)

Observe that

\mathcal{E}\cap\{\omega|_{E(T)}=0\}\subset\{\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}\}\cap\{\omega|_{E(T)}=0\}.

(5.44)

See Figure
6
for an illustration. To estimate the cost of
\{\omega|_{E(T)}=0\}
, we first bound the absolute value field on
V(T)
.
By Proposition
2.6
, there exists a constant
M>0
such that

\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}}[\mathcal{B}]\geq\frac{1}{2}
,
where

\mathcal{B}:=\left\{\sum_{xy\in E(T)}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathsf{a}_{y}\leq M|E(T)|\right\}.

(5.45)

Since
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0,H}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H},\mathsf{a}}
is stochastically dominated by Bernoulli percolation of parameter
p(\beta,\mathsf{a})
,

\displaystyle\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}}[\omega|_{E(T)}=0\mid\mathcal{B}]

\displaystyle\geq\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}}\left[\exp\Big(-2\beta\sum_{xy\in E(T)}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathsf{a}_{y}\Big)\mid\mathcal{B}\right]

(5.46)

\displaystyle\geq e^{-2\beta M|E(T)|}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}}[\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\beta M|E(T)|}\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}}[\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}\mid\mathcal{B}\cap\{\omega|_{E(T)}=0\}]

(5.47)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\beta M|E(T)|}\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}}[\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{B}\cap\{\omega|_{E(T)}=0\}].

We now compare the latter probability with
\Psi^{0,H}_{R(L-\log L,\delta L)}[\mathcal{E}]
.
Using that
e^{\beta\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathsf{a}_{y}}\geq 1
and the definition of the event
\mathcal{B}
we see that

\displaystyle\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}}

\displaystyle[\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{B}\cap\{\omega|_{E(T)}=0\}]

(5.48)

\displaystyle\geq e^{-\beta M|E(T)|}\frac{\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}}[\exp(\sum_{xy\in E(T)}\beta\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathsf{a}_{y})\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\mid\mathcal{B}\cap\{\omega|_{E(T)}=0\}]}{\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}}[\exp(\sum_{xy\in E(T)}\beta\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathsf{a}_{y})\mid\{\omega|_{E(T)}=0\}]}

\displaystyle\geq e^{-\beta M|E(T)|}\frac{\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}}[\exp(\sum_{xy\in E(T)}\beta\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathsf{a}_{y})\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{B}}\mid\{\omega|_{E(T)}=0\}]}{\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}}[\exp(\sum_{xy\in E(T)}\beta\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathsf{a}_{y})\mid\{\omega|_{E(T)}=0\}]}.

By the expression (
4.1
) for the density, the ratio in the last line of (
5.48
) is equal to
\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H},J}[\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{B}]
, where
J_{e}=0
for every
e\in E(T)
, and
J_{e}=1
otherwise. Applying Proposition
2.6
for
\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H},J}
, we can increase the value of
M
, if necessary, so that
\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H},J}[\mathcal{B}]\geq 1-e^{-L^{d-1}}
. At this point, we take cases. Either

\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H},J}[\mathcal{E}]\leq 2\Psi^{0,H}_{R(L,\delta L),J}[\mathcal{B}^{c}]\quad\text{or}\quad\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H},J}[\mathcal{E}]>2\Psi^{0,H}_{R(L,\delta L),J}[\mathcal{B}^{c}].

(5.49)

In the first case, by definition of
J
, the desired result follows directly since we have

\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H},J}[\mathcal{E}]=\Psi^{0,H}_{R(L-\log L,\delta L)}[\mathcal{E}].

(5.50)

In the second case, we have

\displaystyle\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H},J}[\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{B}]

\displaystyle\geq\Psi^{0,H}_{R(L,\delta L),J}[\mathcal{E}]-\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H},J}[\mathcal{B}^{c}]

(5.51)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\Psi^{0}_{R(L,\delta L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H},J}[\mathcal{E}]=\frac{1}{2}\Psi^{0,H}_{R(L-\log L,\delta L)}[\mathcal{E}].

Combining inequalities (
5.41
), (
5.47
), (
5.48
) and (
5.51
) we obtain

\displaystyle\Psi^{0,H}_{R(L-\log L,\delta L)}[\mathcal{E}]

\displaystyle\leq 4e^{3\beta M|E(T)|}\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L),\mathsf{h}^{+,+}_{L,\delta L}+\mathsf{H}}[\mathfrak{g}^{-}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\mathfrak{g}^{+}]

(5.52)

\displaystyle\leq 4e^{3\beta M|E(T)|}e^{-c_{2}/2L^{d-1}}.

(5.53)

Recall that
|E(T)|\leq C\delta^{d-1}L^{d-1}
, and choose
\delta
small enough such that, for
L
large enough

4e^{3\beta M|E(T)|}\leq e^{(c_{2}/4)L^{d-1}}.

(5.54)

Plugging (
5.54
) in (
5.53
), and choosing
c_{3}
small enough to accommodate the small values of
L
concludes the proof.
∎

Finally, we show that we can reduce the probability to free boundary conditions.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\beta>\beta_{c}
. There exist
\delta,c_{4}>0
such that for every
L\geq 1
,

\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}[\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L/2)\longleftrightarrow\partial^{\rm top}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L/2)]\geq 1-e^{-c_{4}L^{d-1}}.

(5.55)

Proof.

Let
h,\delta,c_{3}>0
be the constants of Lemma
5.8
.
Write
\mathcal{D}=\{\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L/2)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\partial^{\rm top}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L/2)\}
.
By inclusion of events, we have

\Psi^{0,h}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}[\mathcal{D}]\leq e^{-c_{3}L^{d-1}}.

(5.56)

We now gradually decrease the value of the external magnetic field from
s=h
to
s=0
in order to interpolate between
\Psi^{0,h}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}[\mathcal{D}]
and
\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}[\mathcal{D}]
.
To this end, consider the derivative with respect to
s
and note that by Russo’s formula (c.f. the calculation of
[
Gri06
, Theorem (2.43)]
in the case of the usual random cluster measure)

\frac{\partial\log\Psi^{0,s}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}[\mathcal{D}]}{\partial s}=\sum_{x\in\partial^{\pm}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)}\Psi^{0,s}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}\left[F_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)\mid\mathcal{D}\right]-\Psi^{0,s}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}\left[F_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)\right],

(5.57)

where
\partial^{\pm}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)=\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)\cup\partial^{\rm top}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)
, and

F_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)=-\beta\mathsf{a}_{x}+\frac{\omega_{x\mathfrak{g}}}{p(\beta,s,\mathsf{a})_{x\mathfrak{g}}(1-p(\beta,s,\mathsf{a})_{x\mathfrak{g}})}\frac{\partial p(\beta,s,\mathsf{a})_{x\mathfrak{g}}}{\partial s}=-\beta\mathsf{a}_{x}\left(1+2\frac{\omega_{x\mathfrak{g}}}{p(\beta,s,\mathsf{a})_{x\mathfrak{g}}}\right).

(5.58)

Here the term
-\beta\mathsf{a}_{x}
arises from differentiating
e^{-\beta\mathsf{a}_{x}s}
, while the other term arises from differentiating
\left(\frac{p(\beta,s,\mathsf{a})}{1-p(\beta,s,\mathsf{a})}\right)^{\omega_{xg}}
.

We are going to split the sum in (
5.57
) according to boundary and bulk contributions. Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\delta/2)
to be fixed below. Define

{\rm Bulk}(\varepsilon,L):=\{x\in\partial^{\pm}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L):{\rm dist}^{\infty}(x,\partial R(L,\delta L)\setminus\partial^{\pm}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L))\geq\varepsilon L\},

(5.59)

and write

\eqref{eq: log derivative}=:I({\rm bulk})+I({\rm boundary})

(5.60)

in the natural way.

Let us treat the boundary contribution first. Arguing as in the proof of (
4.49
) we see that we can replace
2\omega_{x\mathfrak{g}}
by
p(\beta,s,\mathsf{a})_{x\mathfrak{g}}\left(\mathbbm{1}\{x\overset{\omega}{\longleftrightarrow}\mathfrak{g}\}+1\right)
to obtain

I({\rm boundary})=\sum_{x\in{\rm Bulk}(\varepsilon,L)^{c}}\Psi^{0,s}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}\left[G_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)\mid\mathcal{D}\right]-\Psi^{0,s}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}\left[G_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)\right],

(5.61)

where

G_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)=-\beta\mathsf{a}_{x}\left(2+\mathbbm{1}\{x\overset{\omega}{\longleftrightarrow}\mathfrak{g}\}\right).

(5.62)

Notice that
G\leq 0
and
G
is decreasing. Additionally, note that by Proposition
2.6
, there exists
C>0
such that, for every
x\in\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)
,

\max\left(\left|\Psi^{0,s}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}\left[G_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)\right]\right|,\left|\Psi^{0,s}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}\left[G_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)\mid\mathcal{D}\right]\right|\right)\leq C,

(5.63)

where we used the FKG inequality in the second inequality. Hence, there exists
C_{1}>0
such that, for every
s\in(0,h]
,

|I({\rm boundary})|\leq C_{1}\varepsilon L^{d-1}.

(5.64)

We now analyse the bulk contribution, which requires some more care. Without loss of generality, we only treat the case
x\in\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)
.
Define
\mathcal{B}(L,\varepsilon,x):=\Lambda^{+}_{\varepsilon L}(x)=\Lambda_{L}(L\mathbf{e}_{d}+x)
, and for
\mathsf{b}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{\partial\mathcal{B}(L,\varepsilon,x)}
,

\mathsf{h}(x;s,\mathsf{b})_{y}:=\begin{cases}s&\text{for }y\in\partial\mathcal{B}(L,\varepsilon,x)\cap\{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:y_{d}=-\delta L\},\\
\mathsf{b}_{y}&\text{for }y\in\partial\mathcal{B}(L,\varepsilon,x)\cap\{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:y_{d}>-\delta L\},\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(5.65)

See Figure
7
for an illustration. For
\Psi^{0,s}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}
-a.s.
(\omega^{\prime},\mathsf{a}^{\prime})
,

\Psi^{(\xi^{\omega^{\prime}},\mathsf{a}^{\prime})}_{\mathcal{B}(L,\varepsilon,x),\beta}[F_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)]=\Psi^{(\xi^{\omega^{\prime}},\mathsf{a}^{\prime})}_{\mathcal{B}(L,\varepsilon,x),\beta}[G_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)],

(5.66)

where we recall from the Proposition
4.4
that
(\xi^{\omega^{\prime}},\mathsf{a}^{\prime})
is the boundary condition induced by the pair
(\omega^{\prime},\mathsf{a}^{\prime})
on
\mathcal{B}(L,\varepsilon,x)
. Furthermore, note that the event
\mathcal{D}
is supported on
\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L/2)
.
Hence, by using the display above together with Propositions
2.6
and
4.10
, we get that (recall that
G_{x}
is a decreasing function) if
x\in\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)
with
|x|\leq L-\varepsilon L
,

\displaystyle\Psi^{0,s}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}\left[F_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{B}(L,\varepsilon,x),\beta,\mathsf{h}(x;s,\mathfrak{p})}\left[G_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)\right]-o(1),

(5.67)

\displaystyle\Psi^{0,s}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}\left[F_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)\mid\mathcal{D}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{B}(L,\varepsilon,x),\beta,\mathsf{h}(x;s,0)}\left[G_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)\right].

(5.68)

where the
o(1)
term tends to
0
as
L\to\infty
, uniformly over
s\in[0,h]
and
x
as above. Using Proposition
4.15
, one has that
\Psi^{0,s}_{\mathbb{H},\beta}=\Psi^{1,s}_{\mathbb{H},\beta}
for every
s\in(0,h]
. As a result,

\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{B}(L,\varepsilon,x),\beta,\mathsf{h}(x;s,0)}\left[G_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)\right]-\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{B}(L,\varepsilon,x),\beta,\mathsf{h}(x;s,\mathfrak{p})}\left[G_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)\right]=o_{s}(1),

(5.69)

where, for any
s\in(0,h]
,
o_{s}(1)
tends to
0
as
L
tends to infinity uniformly over
x\in\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)
such that
|x|\leq L-\varepsilon L
, and by (
5.74
)
|o_{s}(1)|\leq C
.

Figure 7:
An illustration of the proof of Lemma
5.9
. The blue region is the box
\mathcal{B}(L,\varepsilon,x)=\Lambda^{+}_{\varepsilon L}(x)
. We put an external magnetic field on the bold blue boundary of
\mathcal{B}(L,\delta,x)
which is either
0
or
\mathfrak{p}
. The top and bottom boundaries of
\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)
(in red) carry a magnetic field
s
. The law of
G_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)
within
\mathcal{B}(L,\varepsilon,x)
approximates that of
G_{x}(\mathsf{a},\omega)
under the half-space measures
\Psi^{0,s}_{\mathbb{H},\beta}
or
\Psi^{1,s}_{\mathbb{H},\beta}
. Thanks to Proposition
4.15
, these measures coincide.

Combining the above, we obtain

\Bigg|\frac{\partial\log\Psi^{0,s}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}[\mathcal{D}]}{\partial s}\Bigg|\leq C_{1}\varepsilon L^{d-1}+\sum_{{\rm Bulk}(\varepsilon,L)}o_{s}(1).

(5.70)

Integrating in
s
and using the dominated convergence theorem, we obtain that there exists
C_{2}>0
such that for
L
large enough,

\Big|\log\Psi^{0,h}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}[\mathcal{D}]-\log\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L),\beta}[\mathcal{D}]\Big|\leq C_{2}\varepsilon L^{d-1}.

(5.71)

The proof follows from choosing
\varepsilon
small enough in such a way that
C_{2}\varepsilon\leq c_{3}/2
, and from fixing
c_{4}
small enough to accommodate the small values of
L
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
5.1
.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Lemma
5.9
implies that there exist some
\delta,c_{4}>0
such that for every
L\geq 1
,

\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L)}[\partial\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{bot}}(L,\delta L/2)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\partial\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{top}}(L,\delta L/2)]\leq e^{-c_{4}L^{d-1}}.

(5.72)

Let
\ell\leq\delta L/2
. Note that we can cover the hyperplane
\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}\times\{0\}
by
m\leq C({L}/{\ell})^{d-1}
boxes
\Lambda_{\ell}(x_{1}),\ldots,\Lambda_{\ell}(x_{m})
with
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\in\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-1}\times\{0\}
. With such a choice in hands, we have

\bigcap_{i=1}^{m}\{\Lambda_{\ell}(x_{i})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\partial\Lambda_{\delta L/2}(x_{i})\}\subset\{\partial^{\rm bot}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L/2)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\partial^{\rm top}\mathcal{R}(L,\delta L/2)\}.

(5.73)

Therefore, by the FKG inequality together with (
5.72
), we can find an
i_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
such that

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta}[\Lambda_{\ell}(x_{i_{0}})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\partial\Lambda_{\delta L/2}(x_{i_{0}})]\leq e^{-c_{4}\ell^{d-1}/C}.

(5.74)

By the monotonicity in the volume we obtain

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{2L}(x_{i_{0}}),\beta}[\Lambda_{\ell}(x_{i_{0}})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\partial\Lambda_{\delta L/2}(x_{i_{0}})]\leq\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta}[\Lambda_{\ell}(x_{i_{0}})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}\partial\Lambda_{\delta L/2}(x_{i_{0}})]\leq e^{-c_{4}\ell^{d-1}/C}.

(5.75)

This implies the desired result with
c_{1}=\min\{\delta/2,c_{4}/C\}
.
∎

6
Local uniqueness and renormalisation

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.4
. We handle the dimensions
d=2
and
d\geq 3
separately in Sections
6.1
and
6.2
respectively.

6.1
Proof of Theorem
1.4
for
d=2

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.4
in dimension
d=2
. The proof combines the general Russo–Seymour–Welsh theory developed by Köhler-Schindler and Tassion in
[
KST23
]
with Theorem
5.1
to deduce that open circuits lying in dyadic annuli exist with high probability. For topological reasons, this is enough to obtain the desired local uniqueness.

In order to state the result of
[
KST23
]
in our context, we first introduce some definitions and notation. For
m,n\geq 1
, define the crossing event

\mathcal{C}(m,n)=\left\{\text{
\begin{minipage}[189.75105pt]\centering There exists a path crossing from left to right in $[-m,m]\times[-n,n]$ made of open edges
\@add@centering\end{minipage}}\right\}.

(6.1)

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
d=2
. There exists a continuous and increasing bijection
f:[0,1]\rightarrow[0,1]
such that for every
\beta>0
and every
k\geq 2
we have

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{(k+4)n},\beta}[\mathcal{C}(2n,n)]\geq f\big(\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{kn},\beta}[\mathcal{C}(n,2n)]\big).

(6.2)

for every
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

The result follows from applying
[
KST23
, Theorem 2]
to the measures
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{kn},\beta}
,
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{(k+2)n},\beta}
, and
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{(k+4)n},\beta}
, which satisfy the assumptions of the theorem by the monotonicity of the free measure in the volume.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
1.4
in dimension
d=2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
when
d=2
.

Let
d=2
and
\beta>\beta_{c}
. By Lemma
5.9
, and monotonicity in the volume (see Corollary
4.11
), there exist
\delta>0
such that

\lim_{L\to\infty}\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta}[\mathcal{C}(\delta L,L)]=1.

(6.3)

It is classical to deduce the same statement for
\mathcal{C}(\delta L,2\delta L)
. Theorem
6.1
does the much harder task of going from
\mathcal{C}(\delta L,2\delta L)
to
\mathcal{C}(2\delta L,\delta L)
. Then, it is again classical to construct the local uniqueness event out of
\mathcal{C}(2\delta L,\delta L)
, thus concluding the proof.
The details are given below.

First, notice that if the event
\mathcal{C}(\delta L,L)
holds, then either one of the rectangles of the form
[-\delta L,\delta L]\times[-L+k\delta L,-L+(k+2)\delta L]
for integers
0\leq k\leq 2/\delta-1
is crossed horizontally or one of the squares of the form
[-\delta L,\delta L]\times[-L+k\delta L,-L+(k+1)\delta L]
for
0\leq k\leq 2/\delta-1
is crossed vertically. By the FKG inequality for the complementary events, monotonicity in volume, inclusion of events and the
\pi/2
rotational symmetry

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{2L},\beta}[\mathcal{C}(\delta L,2\delta L)]\geq 1-\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{2L},\beta}[\mathcal{C}(\delta L,L)^{c}]^{\delta/4}\underset{L\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}1.

(6.4)

Then, Theorem
6.1
gives
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{2L+4\delta L},\beta}[\mathcal{C}(2\delta L,\delta L)]\to 1
as
L\to\infty
, which by monotonicity in boundary conditions implies

\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{2L},\beta}[\mathcal{C}(2\eta L,\eta L)]=1,

(6.5)

for some constant
\eta>0
.

Notice that the
U(L)
holds as soon as there exist a circuit surrounding the annulus
\Lambda_{4L}\setminus\Lambda_{2L}
and a crossing from
\Lambda_{L}
to
\partial\Lambda_{8L}
. In particular, one can easily realise
U(L)
as the intersection of a bounded number (depending on
\eta
) of translates and
\pi/2
rotations of the event
\mathcal{C}(2\eta L,\eta L)
within the box
\Lambda_{8L}
. Therefore, by monotonicity and a union bound, the previous display readily implies that
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{10L},\beta}[U(L)]\to 1
as
L\to\infty
, as we wanted to prove.
∎

6.2
Proof of Theorem
1.4
for
d\geq 3

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.4
for
d\geq 3
following the approach of
[
Sev24
]
. The proof is divided into three steps, done in separate subsections. First, we use Theorem
5.1
to show that all macroscopic clusters merge into a unique cluster after sprinkling
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta}
by an independent Bernoulli bond percolation. We then show that increasing the value of
\beta
has a stronger effect than this independent Bernoulli sprinkling of
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta}
. This allows us to deduce that the model percolates in sufficiently thick 2D slabs with minimal (free) boundary conditions. Finally, we use the slab technology to obtain local uniqueness without sprinkling via an onion peeling argument.

6.2.1
Local uniqueness with sprinkling

We consider a measure obtained from
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta}
by sprinkling an independent Bernoulli bond percolation. We will show that in
d\geq 3
, all crossing clusters of
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta}
merge into one cluster after sprinkling.

Let us first introduce some notation. Given
\beta>0
,
\varepsilon>0
, a finite set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and boundary conditions
(\xi,\mathsf{b})
on
\Lambda
,
let
(\omega,\mathsf{a})\sim_{d}\Psi^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda,\beta}
and
\gamma\sim_{d}\mathbb{P}^{\rm Ber}_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
be independent random variables, where

\mathbb{P}^{\rm Ber}_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}=\otimes_{e\in\overline{E}(\Lambda)}\textrm{Ber}(\varepsilon)
denotes the law of independent identically distributed Bernoulli random variables of parameter
\varepsilon
. Denote
\omega\cup\gamma
the configuration on
\overline{E}(\Lambda)
where
(\omega\cup\gamma)_{e}=1
if and only if
\omega_{e}=1
or
\gamma_{e}=1
. Define

{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda,\beta,\varepsilon}:=\Psi^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda,\beta}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{\rm Ber}_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
and let
\Gamma^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda,\beta,\varepsilon}
be the pushforward of
{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda,\beta,\varepsilon}
under the mapping
(\omega,\eta)\mapsto\omega\cup\eta
.

Let
\mathrm{Unique}(L)
be the event that there is a cluster in
\omega\cap\Lambda_{L}
crossing
\Lambda_{L}\setminus\Lambda_{L/8}
and that every cluster of
\omega\cap\Lambda_{L}
crossing
\Lambda_{L/2}\setminus\Lambda_{L/4}
are connected to each other in
(\omega\cup\gamma)\cap\Lambda_{L/2}
.
Our aim is to show that
\mathrm{Unique}(L)
happens with probability tending to
1
as
L
tends to infinity, uniformly over all possible boundary conditions
(\xi,\mathsf{b})
at a macroscopic distance from
\Lambda_{L}
.
The proof adapts the work of Benjamini & Tassion
[
BT17
]
, which concerns the connectedness of “everywhere percolating subsets” of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
after an
\varepsilon
-Bernoulli sprinkling, to the setting of very dense subgraphs. This extension appears in
[
Sev24
]
.

To this end, we first introduce the following notation.
Let
c_{1}>0
be the constant of Theorem
5.1
, and set
\delta:=c_{1}/4
,
C_{0}:=(dc_{1})^{-\frac{1}{d-1}}
, and

\ell=\ell(L,\beta):=C_{0}(\log{L})^{\frac{1}{d-1}}.

(6.6)

Define the event

\mathcal{A}_{L}\coloneqq\bigcap_{x\in\ell\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap\Lambda_{\delta L}}\{\Lambda_{\ell}(x)\overset{\omega}{\longleftrightarrow}\partial\Lambda_{\delta L}\}.

(6.7)

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
d\geq 3
. For every
\beta>\beta_{c}
and every
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\lim_{L\to\infty}\inf_{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}\inf_{\theta\in\mathcal{A}_{L}}{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\varepsilon}[\textup{Unique}(\delta L)\mid\omega=\theta]=1.

(6.8)

Proof.

The proof of this result is given in
[
Sev24
, Proposition 2.2]
. There the result is shown for the standard random cluster measure sprinkled by Bernoulli percolation, but as the desired result is a statement purely about
\gamma
, the same proof applies mutatis mutandis. We note that the proof in
[
Sev24
, Proposition 2.2]
crucially relies on the fact that
\ell=o(\log L)
, this being the only place where
d\geq 3
is used.
∎

With this result in hands we can now deduce that
\mathrm{Unique}(\delta L)
happens with probability tending to
1
under
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\varepsilon}
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
d\geq 3
. For every
\beta>\beta_{c}
and every
\varepsilon>0
we have

\lim_{L\to\infty}\inf_{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\varepsilon}[\textup{Unique}(\delta L)]=1,

(6.9)

where
\delta=c_{1}/4
and
c_{1}
is given by Theorem
5.1
.

Proof.

Let
L^{\prime}\geq 1
and set
L=2L^{\prime}
. By the monotonicity on boundary conditions and Theorem
5.1
, we obtain

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\varepsilon}[\Lambda_{\ell}(x)\overset{\omega}{\longleftrightarrow}\partial\Lambda_{\delta L}]\geq\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{L^{\prime}}}[\Lambda_{\ell}\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{c_{1}L^{\prime}}]\geq 1-L^{-d}

(6.10)

for all
x\in\Lambda_{\delta L}
and all
(\xi,\mathsf{b})
.
By the union bound, we conclude that

\lim_{L\to\infty}\inf_{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\varepsilon}[\mathcal{A}_{L}]=1.

(6.11)

The desired result follows now from Proposition
6.2
.
∎

6.2.2
Slab percolation

Recall that
d\geq 3
. Consider the “slab box” of size
N
and thickness
L
given by

\mathcal{S}(L,N)\coloneqq\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-2}\times\{-N,\ldots,N\}^{2},

(6.12)

and the corresponding “slab” of thickness
L
given by

\mathcal{S}(L)\coloneqq\{-L,\ldots,L\}^{d-2}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

(6.13)

We will show that for every
\beta>\beta_{c}
, there exists a sufficiently large
L
such that
\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{S}(L),\beta}
percolates. We will first show that this is indeed the case once we sprinkle
\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{S}(L),\beta}
with a Bernoulli percolation of an arbitrarily small parameter
\varepsilon>0
. Then, we will show that the edge marginal of the latter sprinkled measure is stochastically dominated by the edge marginal
\Phi^{0}_{\mathcal{S}(L),\beta^{\prime}}
of the measure
\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{S}(L),\beta^{\prime}}
with a slightly higher inverse temperature
\beta^{\prime}
, and deduce that
\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{S}(L),\beta^{\prime}}
percolates. It is important for the next section to state our results in terms of finite-volume measures defined on the slab box
\mathcal{S}(L,N)
. In what follows, given a vertex
x
, we write
\mathrm{Unique}_{x}(L)
for the translation of the event
\mathrm{Unique}(L)
by
x
. We now implement the aforementioned strategy. We first prove the following result.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
d\geq 3
. For every
\beta>\beta_{c}
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
L\geq 1
such that

\inf_{N\geq 0}\inf_{x\in\mathcal{S}(L,N)}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{0}_{\mathcal{S}(L,N),\beta,\varepsilon}[0\overset{\omega\cup\gamma\>}{\longleftrightarrow}x]>0.

Proof.

Since we always have
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{0}_{\mathcal{S}(L,N),\beta,\varepsilon}[0\overset{\omega\cup\gamma\>}{\longleftrightarrow}x]>0
, it suffices to show that the infimum over
N\geq L
is strictly positive.
Let
c_{1}>0
be the constant of Theorem
5.1
, and recall that Proposition
6.3
holds with
\delta=c_{1}/4
. Write
n\coloneqq\lfloor\frac{8(N-L)}{\delta L}\rfloor
, and consider the vertices of the form
x(u)=\left(0_{\bot},\frac{\delta}{8}Lu\right)
for
u\in B_{n}\coloneqq\{-n,\ldots,n\}^{2}
(where
0_{\bot}
is the null element of
\mathbb{Z}^{d-2}
). Note that with this choice, we have
\Lambda_{L}(x(u))\subset\mathcal{S}(L,N)
for all
u\in B_{n}
, and if
|u-v|>16/\delta
, then
\Lambda_{L}(x(u))\cap\Lambda_{L}(x(v))=\emptyset
. We now define a site percolation configuration
\eta
on
B_{n}
by setting
\eta_{u}=1
if and only if the event
\mathrm{Unique}_{x(u)}(\delta L)
happens. First, notice that, by the definition of the event
\mathrm{Unique}(\delta L)
, any path in
\eta
starting at some vertex
u
and ending at some vertex
v
induces a path in
\omega\cup\gamma
from
\Lambda_{\delta L/8}(x(u))
to
\Lambda_{\delta L/8}(x(v))
. Second, by the Markov property, we have

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{0}_{\mathcal{S}(L,N),\beta,\varepsilon}[\eta_{u}=1\mid(\eta_{v}:\,|u-v|>16/\delta)]\geq\alpha(L)~~~\text{a.s. }\forall u\in B_{n},

(6.14)

where
\alpha(L)\coloneqq\inf_{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\mathcal{S}(L,L),\beta,\varepsilon}[\mathrm{Unique}(\delta L)]
, and the infimum is taken over boundary conditions on
\mathcal{S}(L,L)=\Lambda_{L}
. Since
\lim_{L\to\infty}\alpha(L)=1
by Proposition
6.3
, the main result of
[
LSS97
]
implies that there exists an
L\geq 1
such that
\eta
stochastically dominates a Bernoulli site percolation
\mathbb{P}_{s}^{\textup{site}}
on
B_{n}
with parameter
s>p_{c}^{\textup{site}}(\mathbb{Z}^{2})
. Let us fix such an
L
. Since there exists a constant
c>0
such that
\mathbb{P}_{s}^{\textup{site}}[u\longleftrightarrow v]\geq c
for all
u,v\in B_{n}
and all
N
, it follows that

\inf_{N\geq L}\inf_{u,v\in B_{n}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{0}_{\mathcal{S}(L,N),\beta,\varepsilon}[u\overset{\eta}{\longleftrightarrow}v]\geq c.

(6.15)

Now, given any point
x\in\mathcal{S}(L,N)
, there exists
u\in B_{n}
such that
x\in\Lambda_{2L}(x(u))
, so that the event
\{0\overset{\eta}{\longleftrightarrow}u\}\cap\{\Lambda_{L}\text{ fully open in }\gamma\}\cap\{\Lambda_{2L}(x(u))\cap\mathcal{S}(L,N)\text{ fully open in }\gamma\}
is contained in
\{0\overset{\omega\cup\gamma\>}{\longleftrightarrow}x\}
. As a conclusion, conditioning on
\omega
and using the FKG inequality for
\gamma
(note that
\eta
is increasing in
\gamma
) we obtain the desired uniform lower bound
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{0}_{\mathcal{S}(L,N),\beta,\varepsilon}[0\overset{\omega\cup\gamma\>}{\longleftrightarrow}x]\geq c^{\prime}\coloneqq c\varepsilon^{CL^{d}}>0
for every
x\in\mathcal{S}(L,N)
and every
N\geq L
.
∎

Recall from Definition
4.1
that, for
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
\beta>0
, the probability measure
\Phi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta}
on
E(\Lambda)
is the edge-marginal of the free
\varphi^{4}
random cluster measure
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta}
.
Let
\beta>0
and
\beta^{\prime}\in(0,\beta)
. The main technical result of this section is the proof that
\Phi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta}
stochastically dominates the superposition of
\Phi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime}}
and an independent Bernoulli bond percolation
\mathbb{P}^{\rm Ber}_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}
of parameter
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\beta^{\prime},\beta)>0
uniformly in
\Lambda
. This is formalised in the upcoming proposition.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
0<\beta^{\prime}<\beta
. There exists
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\beta^{\prime},\beta)>0
such that for every
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
finite,

\Phi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta}\succcurlyeq\Gamma^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\varepsilon}.

(6.16)

The proof of Proposition
6.5
is rather technical and we defer it to Section
6.3
. We are now ready to prove the main result of this section.

Corollary 6.6
.

Let
d\geq 3
. For every
\beta>\beta_{c}
, there exists
L\geq 1
such that

\inf_{N}\inf_{x\in\mathcal{S}(L,N)}\Psi^{0}_{\mathcal{S}(L,N),\beta}[0\longleftrightarrow x]>0.

(6.17)

Proof.

This follows from applying Proposition
6.4
for some
\beta^{\prime}\in(\beta_{c},\beta)
and
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small so that the stochastic domination of Proposition
6.5
holds.
∎

6.2.3
Uniqueness without sprinkling

We conclude the proof of Theorem
1.4
for
d\geq 3
by showing that the slab percolation statement of Corollary
6.6
implies that the uniqueness (without sprinkling) event
U(L)
happens with high probability, uniformly in boundary conditions. Our proof is based on a classical onion peeling argument, see e.g.
[
Gri99
, Lemma 7.89]
.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
in
d\geq 3
.

Let
\beta>\beta_{c}
. It follows from Propositions
6.3
and
6.5
, and a gluing argument as in the proof of Theorem
1.4
in
d=2
that

\lim_{L\to\infty}\inf_{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}\Psi^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda_{10L},\beta}[\Lambda_{L}\longleftrightarrow\Lambda_{8L}]=1.

(6.18)

It remains to show that with high probability, there is at most one cluster crossing
\Lambda_{4L}\setminus\Lambda_{2L}
. Consider an
L^{\prime}\geq 1
large enough so that Corollary
6.6
holds, and let
n=2L^{\prime}+1
.
First, we partition
\Lambda_{4L}\setminus\Lambda_{2L}
into disjoint annuli of the form
A(k,n)\coloneqq\Lambda_{(k+1)n-1}\setminus\Lambda_{kn-1}
for
\lfloor\frac{2L}{n}\rfloor\leq k\leq\lceil\frac{4L-n}{n}\rceil
. By the domain Markov property, the FKG inequality and the monotonicity in boundary conditions, for any
x,y\in\partial\Lambda_{2L}
and
\lfloor\frac{2L}{n}\rfloor\leq k\leq\lceil\frac{4L-n}{n}\rceil
we have

\displaystyle\Psi^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda_{10L},\beta}

\displaystyle[x\longleftrightarrow y\text{ in }\Lambda_{(k+1)n-1}\mid x\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{kn},y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{kn},x\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}y\text{ in }\Lambda_{kn-1}]

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in\partial\Lambda_{kn-1}}\inf_{(\xi^{\prime},\mathsf{b}^{\prime})}\Psi^{(\xi^{\prime},\mathsf{b}^{\prime})}_{A(k,n),\beta}[u^{\prime}\longleftrightarrow v^{\prime}\text{ in }\Lambda_{(k+1)n-1}\mid u^{\prime}\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{kn},v^{\prime}\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{kn}]

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{u,v\in A(k,n)}\Psi^{0}_{A(k,n),\beta}[u\longleftrightarrow v],

where in the connectivity event of the first inequality we took into account the fact that
\Psi^{(\xi^{\prime},\mathsf{b}^{\prime})}_{A(k,n),\beta}
is a measure on
\overline{E}(A(k,n))\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{A(k,n)}
(see Section
4
).
By Corollary
6.6
and the FKG inequality, there exists a constant
\delta>0
such that
\Psi^{0}_{A(k,n),\beta}[u\longleftrightarrow v]\geq\delta
for any
u,v\in A(k,n)
and any
k
as above. Iterating this bound we see that

\Psi^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda_{10L},\beta}[x\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}y\mid x\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{4L},y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{4L}]\leq(1-\delta)^{2L/n}.

(6.19)

The desired result follows from the union bound over all
x,y\in\partial\Lambda_{2L}
.
∎

6.3
Coupling with a sprinkled measure

In this section we prove Proposition
6.5
. As a first step towards the proof, we show that
\Phi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta}
stochastically dominates the superposition of
\Phi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime}}
and a Bernoulli bond percolation with inhomogeneous
random
edge parameter.
Heuristically, conditioning
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta}
on
\mathsf{a}
gives the usual random cluster measure, but with inhomogeneous edge-weights
p(\mathsf{a},\beta)
defined in (
4.3
).
For these measures, an analogous stochastic domination holds, allowing for the (inhomogeneous) parameter of the independent sprinkling to depend on
\mathsf{a}
by direct calculation. A disintegration argument will then yield the result. Let us now define the latter measure. For every
\mathsf{a}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{\Lambda}
and
xy\in E(\Lambda)
, write

r(\beta,\mathsf{a})_{xy}=\frac{1-e^{-2\beta\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathsf{a}_{y}}}{1+e^{-2\beta\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathsf{a}_{y}}}.

(6.20)

Denote
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta}
the measure on
\{0,1\}^{E(\Lambda)}\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{\Lambda}

defined by

\mathrm{d}\Psi_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta}^{0}[(\omega,\mathsf{a})]=\gamma^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}[\omega]\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime}}^{0}(\mathsf{a}),

(6.21)

where
\gamma^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}
is the superposition of
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a}}
and an independent Bernoulli bond percolation on
E(\Lambda)
with law
\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}^{\rm Ber}
defined by

\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}^{\rm Ber}[\omega]=\prod_{xy\in E(\Lambda)}r(\beta-\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{xy}^{\omega_{xy}}(1-r(\beta-\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a}))_{xy}^{1-\omega_{xy}}.

(6.22)

Finally, write
\Gamma^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta}
for the projection of
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta}
on to
\{0,1\}^{E(\Lambda)}
.

In order to state the intermediate stochastic domination result, we will need the following notation. Given
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, let
G=(\Lambda,E^{1,2})
be the graph with vertex set
\Lambda
, where for each edge
xy\in E(\Lambda)
,
G
contains two parallel edges
xy(1)
and
xy(2)
. We write
E^{i}=\{xy(i):xy\in E(\Lambda)\}
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
0<\beta^{\prime}<\beta
. For every
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
finite,
\Phi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta}\succcurlyeq\Gamma^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{a}\sim\mu^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime}}
. We write

\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0}_{G,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}
for the random cluster measure on
G
defined by

\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0}_{G,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}[\omega]=\frac{1}{Z^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}}\prod_{xy\in E(\Lambda)}\Big(\frac{p(\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{xy}}{1-p(\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{xy}}\Big)^{\omega_{xy(1)}}\Big(\frac{p(\beta-\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{xy}}{1-p(\beta-\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{xy}}\Big)^{\omega_{xy(2)}}\,2^{k^{0}(\omega)}.

(6.23)

Consider also the product measure
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a}}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{\rm Ber}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}
, which we view as a measure on
\{0,1\}^{E^{1,2}}
in the natural way.

We first show that
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0}_{G,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}
stochastically dominates
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a}}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{\rm Ber}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}
. Consider configurations
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{E^{1,2}}
such that
\theta\geq\theta^{\prime}
. A direct calculation gives

\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0}_{G,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}[\omega_{xy(1)}=1\mid\omega|_{E^{1,2}\setminus\{xy(1)\}}=\theta|_{E^{1,2}\setminus\{xy(1)\}}]=\begin{cases}p(\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{xy}&\text{ if $x\overset{\theta|_{E^{1,2}\setminus\{xy(1)\}}}{\longleftrightarrow}y$,}\\
\displaystyle r(\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{xy}&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

(6.24)

and

\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0}_{G,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}[\omega_{xy(2)}=1\mid\omega|_{E^{1,2}\setminus\{xy(2)\}}=\theta|_{E^{1,2}\setminus\{xy(2)\}}]=\begin{cases}p(\beta-\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{xy}&\text{ if $x\overset{\theta|_{E^{1,2}\setminus\{xy(2)\}}}{\longleftrightarrow}y$,}\\
\displaystyle r(\beta-\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{xy}&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(6.25)

A similar calculation combined with the fact that
p(t,\mathsf{a})\geq r(t,\mathsf{a})
for
t\geq 0
shows that
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0}_{G,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}[\omega_{xy(1)}=1\mid\omega|_{E^{1,2}\setminus\{xy(1)\}}=\theta|_{E^{1,2}\setminus\{xy(1)\}}]
is at least
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a}}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{\rm Ber}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta.\mathsf{a}}[\omega_{xy(1)}=1\mid\omega|_{E^{1,2}\setminus\{xy(1)\}}=\theta^{\prime}|_{E^{1,2}\setminus\{xy(1)\}}]
. A standard Markov chain argument then implies that
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0}_{G,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}
stochastically dominates
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}
. See e.g.
[
Dum19
, Lemma 1.5]
.

Let
\omega^{\max}\in\{0,1\}^{E(\Lambda)}
be the configuration defined by

\omega^{\max}_{xy}=\max\{\omega_{xy(1)},\omega_{xy(2)}\}
. Notice that if
\omega\sim\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0}_{G,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}
, then
\omega^{\max}
is distributed according to
\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathsf{a}}
.
The desired result now follows from the fact that
\mu_{\Lambda,\beta}^{0}
stochastically dominates
\mu_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime}}^{0}
and the monotonicity of the random cluster and Bernoulli percolation measures in the edge parameter.
∎

Note that the parameter
r(\beta-\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{xy}
is close to
0
when
\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathsf{a}_{y}
is close to
0
. Thanks to Lemma
6.7
, in order to prove Proposition
6.5
it will suffice to show that
\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathsf{a}_{y}
remains bounded away from
0
with good
\mu^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime}}
-probability when we condition on the state of all edges. We will begin by showing that, conditionally on the state of all edges,
\mathsf{a}_{x}
remains bounded with good probability for every vertex
x
. For technical reasons that will become clear later, we will work with a slightly more general family of measures, where we ignore the contribution coming from certain closed edges.

As above, let us fix
0<\beta^{\prime}<\beta
and
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
finite. Let
A,B\subset E(\Lambda)
, and
\omega^{1},\omega^{2}\in\{0,1\}^{E(\Lambda)}
. Denote by
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}(\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta,A,B,\omega^{1},\omega^{2})
the probability measure on
(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{\Lambda}
defined by the Radon–Nikodym derivative

d\boldsymbol{\mu}(\mathsf{a})=\frac{1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}\boldsymbol{\phi}^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a}}[\omega^{1}]\mathbb{P}^{\rm Ber}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\mathsf{a}}[\omega^{2}|_{E(\Lambda)\setminus B}]\prod_{xy\in A}e^{\beta^{\prime}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathsf{a}_{y}}\mathrm{d}\mu^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a}),

(6.26)

where
\boldsymbol{Z}
is a normalisation constant and we recall from (
4.8
) that

\mathrm{d}\mu^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=\frac{\mathbf{Z}^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a}}}{Z^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime}}}\prod_{xy\in E(\Lambda)}e^{-\beta^{\prime}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathsf{a}_{y}}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\mathrm{d}\rho_{g,a}(\mathsf{a}_{x}).

(6.27)

Note that when
A=B=\emptyset
, then
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is the projection of the conditional measure
\Psi^{0}_{G,\beta^{\prime},\beta}[\>\cdot\mid\omega|_{E^{1}}=\omega^{1},\omega|_{E^{2}}=\omega^{2}]
to
(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{\Lambda}
, where
G=(\Lambda,E^{1,2})
is as above and
by abuse of notation we write
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta}
to denote its natural lift to the multigraph
G
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
0<\beta^{\prime}<\beta
. There exists a constant
C=C(\beta^{\prime},\beta,d)>1
such that for every
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
finite, every
A,B\subset E(\Lambda)
, every pair of configurations
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{E(\Lambda)}
, and every
x\in\Lambda
we have

\boldsymbol{\mu}[\mathsf{a}_{x}\geq s]\leq C\int_{s}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{g}{2}t^{4}-at^{2}}\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\forall s\geq C.

(6.28)

Proof.

Fix a vertex
x\in\Lambda
and let
C>1
be a sufficiently large constant to be determined. For
s\geq C^{3}
, let
E_{s}=E_{s}(x)
be the event that
\mathsf{a}_{y}\leq\max\{s^{d(x,y)+1},C\max_{z\sim y}\{\mathsf{a}_{z}^{1/3}\}\}
for every
y\in\Lambda
.
We claim that on the event
E_{s}
,
\mathsf{a}_{x}\leq s
. Indeed, consider a configuration
\mathsf{a}\in E_{s}
, and let
y\in\Lambda
be such that
\mathsf{a}_{y}/s^{d(x,y)+1}
is maximal. Then by definition of the event
E_{s}
,

\displaystyle M:=\frac{\mathsf{a}_{y}}{s^{d(x,y)+1}}

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{1,\frac{C}{s^{2(d(x,y)+1)/3}}\max_{z\sim y}\left\{\left(\frac{\mathsf{a}_{z}}{s^{d(x,y)+1}}\right)^{1/3}\right\}\right\}

(6.29)

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{1,\frac{Cs^{1/3}}{s^{2(d(x,y)+1)/3}}\max_{z\sim y}\left\{\left(\frac{\mathsf{a}_{z}}{s^{d(x,z)+1}}\right)^{1/3}\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{1,\frac{Cs^{1/3}}{s^{2(d(x,y)+1)/3}}M^{1/3}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\max\{1,M^{1/3}\}.

Now either
M\leq 1
or
M\leq M^{1/3}
. In both cases,
M\leq 1
. This implies in particular that
\mathsf{a}_{x}\leq s
, as claimed.

Thus, it suffices to estimate the probability of
E_{s}
happening for some constant
C>1
large enough. We will prove this by comparing the value of the density
\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(\mathsf{a})
of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
for different values of
\mathsf{a}
. To this end,
for each edge
uv\in E(\Lambda)
, let

a_{uv}(\mathsf{a}_{u},\mathsf{a}_{v})=\begin{cases}e^{-\beta^{\prime}\mathsf{a}_{u}\mathsf{a}_{v}}\Big(\frac{p(\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{uv}}{1-p(\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{uv}}\Big)^{\omega^{1}_{uv}},&uv\not\in A\\
1,&uv\in A\end{cases}

(6.30)

and

b_{uv}(\mathsf{a}_{u},\mathsf{a}_{v})=\begin{cases}r(\beta-\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})^{\omega^{2}_{uv}}_{uv}\left(1-r(\beta-\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{uv}\right)^{1-\omega^{2}_{uv}},&uv\not\in B\\
1,&uv\in B,\end{cases}

(6.31)

where
r(\beta-\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})
is as above. We first compare the values of
a_{uv}(\mathsf{a}_{u},\mathsf{a}_{v})
and
b_{uv}(\mathsf{a}_{u},\mathsf{a}_{v})
when the first argument
\mathsf{a}_{u}\in[1,2]
and when
\mathsf{a}_{u}
is large, where in both instances we consider
\mathsf{a}_{v}
fixed. Let
f(t)=\log(1-e^{-t})
and note that
f(2\beta^{\prime}\mathsf{a}_{u}\mathsf{a}_{v})=\log p(\mathsf{a},\beta^{\prime})_{uv}
.

For
t>0
,

f^{\prime}(t)=\frac{e^{-t}}{1-e^{-t}}=\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}\leq\frac{1}{t},

(6.32)

since
e^{t}\geq 1+t
. By integrating we obtain that for
\mathsf{a}_{u}\geq 2
and
t\in[1,2]

f(2\beta^{\prime}\mathsf{a}_{u}\mathsf{a}_{v})-f(2\beta^{\prime}t\mathsf{a}_{v})\leq\log(2\beta^{\prime}\mathsf{a}_{u}\mathsf{a}_{v})-\log(2\beta^{\prime}t\mathsf{a}_{v})\leq\log(\mathsf{a}_{u}).

(6.33)

Hence
p(\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{uv}\leq\mathsf{a}_{u}(1-e^{-2\beta^{\prime}t\mathsf{a}_{v}})
.
By taking cases according to whether
uv\in A
or not, and whether
\omega^{1}_{uv}=1
or not, we see that in any case have

a_{uv}(\mathsf{a}_{u},\mathsf{a}_{v})\leq e^{\beta^{\prime}\mathsf{a}_{u}\mathsf{a}_{v}}\mathsf{a}_{u}a_{uv}(t,\mathsf{a}_{v})

(6.34)

for every
\mathsf{a}_{u}\geq 2
and
t\in[1,2]
.

We now turn to
b_{uv}(\mathsf{a}_{u},\mathsf{a}_{v})
. Let
g(t)=\log(1+e^{-t})
and note that
\log r(\beta-\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})=f(2(\beta-\beta^{\prime})\mathsf{a}_{u}\mathsf{a}_{v})-g(2(\beta-\beta^{\prime})\mathsf{a}_{u}\mathsf{a}_{v})
.
For
t>0
we have

g^{\prime}(t)=-\frac{e^{-t}}{1+e^{-t}}=-\frac{1}{e^{t}+1}\geq-\frac{1}{t}.

(6.35)

Hence for every
\mathsf{a}_{u}\geq 2
and
t\in[1,2]
we have

\exp g(2(\beta-\beta^{\prime})\mathsf{a}_{u}\mathsf{a}_{v})\geq\mathsf{a}_{u}^{-1}\exp g(2(\beta-\beta^{\prime})t\mathsf{a}_{v})
, and hence
r(\beta-\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})\leq\mathsf{a}_{u}^{2}\frac{1-e^{-2\beta^{\prime}t\mathsf{a}_{v}}}{1+e^{-2\beta^{\prime}t\mathsf{a}_{v}}}.

Thus, by examining each case separately, we have

b_{uv}(\mathsf{a}_{u},\mathsf{a}_{v})\leq\mathsf{a}_{u}^{2}b_{uv}(t,\mathsf{a}_{v}).

(6.36)

Now if
\mathsf{a}_{u}\geq C\mathsf{a}_{v}^{1/3}
for
C=\sqrt[3]{\frac{8\beta^{\prime}d}{g}}
, then

e^{\beta^{\prime}\mathsf{a}_{u}\mathsf{a}_{v}}\leq e^{\frac{g}{8d}\mathsf{a}_{u}^{4}}.

(6.37)

By increasing the value of
C
if necessary, we can further ensure that

\mathsf{a}_{u}^{3}e^{-\frac{g}{8d}\mathsf{a}_{u}^{4}}\leq\min\{1,e^{-gt^{4}-at^{2}}\}

(6.38)

for every
\mathsf{a}_{u}\geq C
and every
t\in[1,2]
. Since
u
has degree at most
2d
, combining inequalities (
6.34
), (
6.36
), (
6.37
) and (
6.38
) we get

\displaystyle\prod_{v\sim u}a_{uv}(\mathsf{a}_{u},\mathsf{a}_{v})b_{uv}(\mathsf{a}_{u},\mathsf{a}_{v})e^{-g\mathsf{a}_{u}^{4}-a\mathsf{a}_{u}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{g}{2}\mathsf{a}_{u}^{4}-a\mathsf{a}_{u}^{2}}\min_{t\in[1,2]}\left\{\prod_{v\sim u}a_{uv}(t,\mathsf{a}_{v})b_{uv}(t,\mathsf{a}_{v})e^{-gt^{4}-at^{2}}\right\}

(6.39)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{g}{2}\mathsf{a}_{u}^{4}-a\mathsf{a}_{u}^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{v\sim u}a_{uv}(t,\mathsf{a}_{v})b_{uv}(t,\mathsf{a}_{v})e^{-gt^{4}-at^{2}}\mathrm{d}t

for every
\mathsf{a}_{u}\geq C\max\{1,\max_{v\sim u}\{\mathsf{a}_{v}^{1/3}\}\}
.
By integrating and letting
E_{u,s}=\{\mathsf{a}_{u}\leq\max\{s^{d(x,u)+1},C\max_{v\sim u}\{\mathsf{a}_{v}^{1/3}\}\}\}
for
s\geq C
, we obtain that

\boldsymbol{\mu}[E_{u,s}^{c}]\leq\int_{s^{d(x,u)+1}}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{g}{2}t^{4}-at^{2}}\mathrm{d}t.

(6.40)

It is not hard to see that there exists a constant
c>0
such that for
C>1
large enough and every
s\geq C
and
k\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\int_{s^{k}}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{g}{2}t^{4}-at^{2}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s^{k}}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{g}{4}t^{4}-\frac{a}{2}t^{2}}e^{-\frac{g}{2}t^{4/k}-at^{2/k}}\mathrm{d}t

(6.41)

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{\infty}kt^{\frac{k-1}{k}}e^{-\frac{g}{4}t^{4k}-\frac{a}{2}t^{2k}}e^{-\frac{g}{2}t^{4}-at^{2}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq e^{-c(k-1)}\int_{s}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{g}{2}t^{4}-at^{2}}\mathrm{d}t.

The desired result now follows (up to a redefinition of
C
as necessary) from (
6.40
) and (
6.41
) and a union bound over all vertices
u
in
\Lambda
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
6.5
.

Proof of Proposition
6.5
.

Consider two neighbouring vertices
x,y\in\Lambda
, and let
\omega_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{E(\Lambda)}
,
\omega_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{E(\Lambda)\setminus\{xy\}}
. Our aim is to show that

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta}[\omega_{xy(2)}=1\mid\omega|_{E^{1}}=\omega_{1},\omega|_{E^{2}\setminus xy(2)}=\omega_{2}]\geq\varepsilon

(6.42)

for some constant
\varepsilon>0
that depends only on
\beta^{\prime}
and
\beta
. Assuming that (
6.42
) holds, we can deduce that the projection of
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta}
on
E^{2}
stochastically dominates a Bernoulli bond percolation on
E(\Lambda)
which is independent from the projection of
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta}
on
E^{1}
. The desired result then follows from Lemma
6.7
.

It remains to prove (
6.42
).
Let
S_{i}
be the set of edges
uv
with
u\in\{x,y\}
such that
\omega^{i}_{uv}=0
, and let
A=S_{1}
and
B=S_{2}\cup\{xy\}
.
It is not hard to see that for
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}(\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta,A,B,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
, the Radon–Nikodym derivative

\frac{\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mu}(\mathsf{a})}{\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\mathrm{d}\rho_{g,a}(\mathsf{a}_{x})}=\frac{1}{Z}\prod_{uv\in E(\Lambda)}a_{uv}(\mathsf{a}_{u},\mathsf{a}_{v})b_{uv}(\mathsf{a}_{u},\mathsf{a}_{v})=:f(\mathsf{a})

(6.43)

is an increasing function of
\mathsf{a}_{x}
and
\mathsf{a}_{y}
, where
a_{uv}
and
b_{uv}
are defined in (
6.30
) and (
6.31
), and where
Z=Z(\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta,A,B,\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
is a normalisation constant. Indeed, this follows from the fact that the functions
a_{uv}
and
b_{uv}
are increasing for all edges
uv
such that
u\in\{x,y\}
. Let
C>1
a large enough constant to be determined. Define

F(\mathsf{a}_{x},\mathsf{a}_{y}):=\int_{(\mathbb{R})^{\Lambda\setminus\{x,y\}}}\mathbbm{1}_{G_{x,y}}f(\mathsf{a})\prod_{x\in\Lambda\setminus\{x,y\}}\mathrm{d}\rho_{g,a}(\mathsf{a}_{x}),

(6.44)

where
G_{x,y}
is the event that
\mathsf{a}_{u}\leq C
for every
u
which either lies in
\{x,y\}
or has a neighbour in
\{x,y\}
. Note that since
f(\mathsf{a})
is increasing in
\mathsf{a}_{x}
and
\mathsf{a}_{y}
, the function
F
is increasing.

Using the absolute value FKG for the product measure
\rho^{\otimes 2,\leq C}:=\rho_{g,a}[\>\cdot\mid\mathsf{a}_{x}\leq C]\otimes\rho_{g,a}[\>\cdot\mid\mathsf{a}_{y}\leq C]
, which is a special case of Proposition
4.8
for
\beta=0
, we obtain that

\boldsymbol{\mu}[\mathsf{a}_{x}\geq 1,\mathsf{a}_{y}\geq 1\mid G_{x,y}]=\frac{\rho^{\otimes 2,\leq C}[F(\mathsf{a}_{x},\mathsf{a}_{y})\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{a}_{x}\geq 1,\>\mathsf{a}_{y}\geq 1}]}{\rho^{\otimes 2,\leq C}[F(\mathsf{a}_{x},\mathsf{a}_{y})]}\geq\left(\rho_{g,a}[\mathsf{a}\geq 1\mid\mathsf{a}\leq C]\right)^{2},

(6.45)

On the event
G_{x,y}
we have that
1-r(\beta-\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{uv}
and
e^{-\beta^{\prime}\mathsf{a}_{u}\mathsf{a}_{v}}
are bounded away from
0
for every edge
uv
with
u\in\{x,y\}
, while on the event
\{\mathsf{a}_{x}\geq 1,\mathsf{a}_{y}\geq 1\}
we have
r(\beta-\beta^{\prime},\mathsf{a})_{xy}
is bounded away from
0
. Writing
S(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})=\{\omega|_{E^{1}}=\omega_{1},\omega|_{E^{2}\setminus xy(2)}=\omega_{2}\}
, we can now deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta}[\omega_{xy(2)}=1\mid S(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})]}{\boldsymbol{\mu}[G_{x,y}]}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta}[\omega_{xy(2)}=1,\mathsf{a}_{x}\geq 1,\mathsf{a}_{y}\geq 1,G_{x,y}\mid S(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})]}{\boldsymbol{\mu}[G_{x,y}]}

(6.46)

\displaystyle\geq\delta\boldsymbol{\mu}[\mathsf{a}_{x}\geq 1,\mathsf{a}_{y}\geq 1\mid G_{x,y}]

\displaystyle\geq\delta\left(\rho_{g,a}[\mathsf{a}\geq 1\mid\mathsf{a}\leq C]\right)^{2}

for some constant
\delta>0
depending only on
\beta^{\prime}
and
\beta
, where we used that once the contribution of the closed edges in
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
is removed from
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta^{\prime},\beta}[\>\cdot\mid S(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})]
, we obtain a measure whose projection on
\mathsf{a}
coincides with
\boldsymbol{\mu}
. By Lemma
6.8
and a union bound, there exists
C>1
large enough so that
\boldsymbol{\mu}[G_{x,y}]\geq 1/2
.
The desired inequality (
6.42
) follows readily.
∎

7
Surface order large deviations

In this section, we prove the surface order (lower) large deviations of Theorem
1.1
, which will follow rather easily from Lemmas
7.1
and
7.2
and the key Proposition
7.4
below. The latter is proved by combining Theorem
1.4
and a Pisztora’s coarse graining.
We also show the volume order (upper) large deviations mentioned in Remark
1.2
, which follows from Lemma
7.3
.

The following simple lemma allows us to disregard large absolute values of the field.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\beta\geq 0
and
\delta>0
. There exists
M_{0}=M_{0}(\delta)
such that, for every
M\geq M_{0}
and every
n
large enough,

\displaystyle\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\left[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{a}_{x}\geq M}\geq\delta\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{-n^{d}}.

(7.1)

Proof.
