By Proposition
2.6
, the field
(\mathsf{a}_{x})_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}
is stochastically dominated by a field
\mathsf{a}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{a}^{\prime}_{x})_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}
with independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) marginals with quartic exponential tails and, in particular, finite exponential moments. Since the event in (
7.1
) is increasing, it is enough to prove the bound for the i.i.d. field
\mathsf{a}^{\prime}
. This follows from classical large deviations results, see e.g.
[
Dur19
, Theorem 2.7.7]
.
∎

The next lemma allows us to control the lower large deviations for vertices belonging to large clusters.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
\beta\geq 0
. For every
\delta>0
and
K\geq 1
, there exists
c=c(K,\delta)>0
such that, for every boundary condition
(\xi,\mathsf{b})
, and for every
n
large enough,

\Psi_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}\left[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{|\mathcal{C}_{x}|\geq K}\leq m^{*}(\beta)-\delta\right]\leq e^{-cn^{d}}.

(7.2)

Proof.

We can always assume that
m^{*}(\beta)>\delta
(in particular
\beta>\beta_{c})
. We fix
K\geq 0
. For
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we let
Y_{x}^{K}:=\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{|\mathcal{C}_{x}|\geq K}
. First of all, notice that, since the event in (
7.2
) is decreasing, it is sufficient to prove the above statement for the measure
\Psi_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}^{0}
. Using Remark
4.14
, one has that, for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

\Psi^{0}_{\beta}[\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{0\leftrightarrow\infty}]=\Psi^{1}_{\beta}[\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{0\leftrightarrow\infty}]=m^{*}(\beta).

(7.3)

As a result, for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

\Psi^{0}_{\beta}[Y_{x}^{K}]\geq m^{*}(\beta).

(7.4)

Moreover, since
Y_{x}^{K}
is a local increasing function measurable with respect to
\Lambda_{K}(x)
, there exists
L=L(K,\delta)>K
such that, for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{L}(x),\beta}[Y_{x}^{K}]\geq m^{*}(\beta)-\frac{\delta}{2}.

(7.5)

Note that by Proposition
2.6
, the random variable
Y_{0}^{K}
has exponential tails (under the measure
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{L}}
).
Choose
n\geq 10L
. Let
\mathbb{P}
be the law of a collection of i.i.d. random variables
(\tilde{Y}_{x}^{K})_{x\in\Lambda_{n-2L}}
of law given by the law of
Y_{0}^{K}
under
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta}
. Let
m:=\lfloor\tfrac{n-2L}{4L}\rfloor
.
As a consequence of monotonicity in boundary conditions, we find that, for any
y\in\Lambda_{4L}
, the collection of random variables
(Y_{x}^{K})_{x\in(y+4L\Lambda_{m})}
under the measure
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}
stochastically dominates
(\tilde{Y}_{x}^{K})_{x\in(y+4L\Lambda_{m})}
. For
y\in\Lambda_{4L}
, introduce the (decreasing) event

\mathcal{E}_{y}(Y^{K}):=\left\{\frac{1}{|y+4L\Lambda_{m}|}\sum_{x\in(y+4L\Lambda_{m})}Y^{K}_{x}\leq m^{*}(\beta)-\frac{3\delta}{4}\right\}.

(7.6)

Similarly, one may define
\mathcal{E}_{w}(\tilde{Y}^{K}).
Classical large deviations estimates for independent random variables with exponential tails (see
[
Dur19
, Theorem 2.7.7]
) imply the existence of
c_{1}=c_{1}(K,\delta)>0
such that, for every
n
large enough,

\sup_{y\in\Lambda_{4L}}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{y}(\tilde{Y}^{K})]\leq e^{-c_{1}n^{d}}.

(7.7)

Now, letting
n
be large enough so that
\tfrac{|\Lambda_{n}|}{|\Lambda_{n-2L}|}\leq 1+\tfrac{\delta/4}{m^{*}(\beta)-\delta}
, there exists
c_{2}=c_{2}(K,\delta)>0
such that,

\displaystyle\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\left[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}Y_{x}^{K}\leq m^{*}(\beta)-\delta\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\left[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n-2L}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n-2L}}Y_{x}^{K}\leq m^{*}(\beta)-\frac{3\delta}{4}\right]

(7.8)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{w\in\Lambda_{4L}}\Psi_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}^{0}[\mathcal{E}_{w}(Y^{K})]

(7.9)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{w\in\Lambda_{4L}}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{w}(\tilde{Y}^{K})]\leq e^{-c_{2}n^{d}},

(7.10)

where we used the stochastic domination in the third line, and (
7.7
) in the last line. This concludes the proof.
∎

The next lemma is the upper deviation counterpart of Lemma
7.2
. It will only be used to prove the upper large deviations stated in Remark
1.2
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\beta\geq 0
. For every
\delta>0
, there exist
K_{1}=K_{1}(\delta)\geq 1
and
c=c(\delta)>0
such that for every
K\geq K_{1}
, and for every
n
large enough,

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\Bigg[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{|\mathcal{C}_{x}|\geq K}\geq m^{*}(\beta)+\delta\Bigg]\leq e^{-cn^{d}}.

(7.11)

Proof.

We will argue as in the proof of the previous lemma, with certain additional complexities arising from the presence of large absolute values. Again, for
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we let
Y_{x}^{K}=\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{|\mathcal{C}_{x}|\geq K}
. We first consider
\Psi^{1}_{\beta}
and we note that there exists
K>0
such that, for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

\Psi^{1}_{\beta}[Y_{x}^{K}]\leq m^{*}(\beta)+\frac{\delta}{8}.

(7.12)

Since
Y_{x}^{K}
is a local function (around
x
), there exists
L>K
large enough such that, for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

\Psi^{(w,\mathfrak{p})}_{\Lambda_{L}(x),\beta}[Y_{x}^{K}]\leq m^{*}(\beta)+\frac{\delta}{4}.

(7.13)

We now consider
n\geq 10L
, and let
y\in\Lambda_{4L}
. We consider the collection of boxes of the form
\Lambda_{L}(y+4Lz)
for
z\in\Lambda_{m}
, where
m=\lfloor\frac{n-2L}{4L}\rfloor
. Note that the union of all these boxes over
y\in\Lambda_{4L}
and
z\in\Lambda_{m}
covers
\Lambda_{n-5L}
. Recall the definition of
\mathfrak{p}
. For each
y\in\Lambda_{4L}
and
z\in\Lambda_{m}
, let
B_{z}
denote the event
\{\exists z\in\partial\Lambda_{L}(y+4Lz)\text{ such that }\mathsf{a}_{z}\geq C_{M}(1\vee\log|\Lambda_{L}|)^{1/4}\}
. With this definition in hand, we write

\displaystyle\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\Bigg[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}Y_{x}^{K}\geq

\displaystyle\>m^{*}(\beta)+\delta\Bigg]\leq\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\Bigg[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}\setminus\Lambda_{n-5L}}Y_{x}^{K}\geq\frac{\delta}{3}\Bigg]

(7.14)

\displaystyle+\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{4L}}\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\Bigg[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{m}|}\sum_{z\in\Lambda_{m}}Y_{y+4Lz}^{K}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{z}}\geq\frac{\delta}{3}\Bigg]

(7.15)

\displaystyle+\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{4L}}\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\Bigg[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{m}|}\sum_{z\in\Lambda_{m}}Y_{y+4Lz}^{K}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{z}^{c}}\geq m^{*}(\beta)+\frac{\delta}{3}\Bigg],

(7.16)

and we bound each probability separately.

We begin with the term on the right-hand side of (
7.14
). Let
M_{0}=M_{0}(\delta/3)
be given by Lemma
7.1
. If
n
is large enough so that
\tfrac{|\Lambda_{n}\setminus\Lambda_{n-5L}|}{|\Lambda_{n}|}M_{0}<\tfrac{\delta}{3}
, it follows from Lemma
7.1
that

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\Bigg[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}\setminus\Lambda_{n-5L}}Y_{x}^{K}\geq\frac{\delta}{3}\Bigg]\leq\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\left[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{a}_{x}\geq M_{0}}\geq\frac{\delta}{3}\right]\leq e^{-n^{d}}.

(7.17)

To estimate the term in (
7.15
),
recall that
\mathsf{a}\sim\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}
is stochastically dominated by a sequence of i.i.d. random variables
\mathsf{a}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{a}^{\prime}_{x})_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}
that have quartic tails. Writing
\mathbb{P}
for the law of these random variables, we see that— to the cost of potentially increasing the value of
L
— we can assume that

\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{a}^{\prime}_{y+4Lz}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{z}}]\leq\tfrac{\delta}{4}
for every
z\in\Lambda_{m}
. Since for fixed
y
, the random variables
\mathsf{a}^{\prime}_{y+4Lz}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{z}}
are independent under
\mathbb{P}
, it follows again from classical large deviations estimates that there exists
c_{1}>0
such that, for every
n
large enough and every
y\in\Lambda_{4L}
,

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\Bigg[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{m}|}\sum_{z\in\Lambda_{m}}Y_{y+4Lz}^{K}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{z}}\geq\frac{\delta}{3}\Bigg]\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{m}|}\sum_{z\in\Lambda_{m}}\mathsf{a}^{\prime}_{y+4Lz}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{z}}\geq\frac{\delta}{3}\Bigg]\leq e^{-c_{1}n^{d}}.

(7.18)

Finally, we estimate the term in (
7.16
). We use the monotonicity in the boundary conditions to deduce that for each
y\in\Lambda_{4L}
, the collection of random variables
(Y_{y+4Lz}^{K})_{z\in\Lambda_{m}}
is stochastically dominated by a sequence of independent random variables
(\tilde{Y}^{K}_{y+4Lz})_{z\in\Lambda_{m}}
where each marginal is distributed according to the law of
Y_{y}^{K}
under
\Psi^{(w,\mathfrak{p})}_{\Lambda_{L}(y),\beta}
, and in particular have exponential tails by Proposition
2.6
. By (
7.13
), the latter have mean at most
m^{*}(\beta)+\tfrac{\delta}{4}
. Hence, we can use again the large deviation estimates for independent random variables with exponential tails (see
[
Dur19
]
) to obtain that for some
c_{2}>0
and for every
n
large enough,

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\Bigg[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{m}|}\sum_{z\in\Lambda_{m}}Y_{y+4Lz}^{K}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{z}^{c}}\geq m^{*}(\beta)+\delta/3\Bigg]\leq e^{-c_{2}n^{d}}.

(7.19)

Plugging (
7.17
)–(
7.19
) in (
7.14
)–(
7.16
) concludes the proof.
∎

Let
\mathfrak{C}=\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{n})
be the set of all clusters in
\Lambda_{n}
. Define
\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}
to be the element of
\mathfrak{C}
of largest cardinality (with an arbitrary rule to break ties).
We can now state the main crucial ingredient for the proof of Theorem
1.1
. This proposition allows us to deal with the contributions of large clusters which are not the (unique) giant one.
The proof uses Theorem
1.4
and a coarse graining argument in order to reduce the problem to a highly supercritical Bernoulli percolation.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
\beta>\beta_{c}
. For every
\delta>0
, there exist
N_{0}=N_{0}(\delta)\geq 1
and
c=c(\delta)>0
such that, for every boundary condition
(\xi,\mathsf{b})
, for every
N\geq N_{0}
, and for every
n
large enough,

\Psi^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\Bigg[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}\setminus\{\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}\}\\
|\mathcal{C}|\geq N\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{C}|\geq\delta\Bigg]\leq e^{-cn^{d-1}}.

(7.20)

We will use the following result about the largest cluster in highly supercritical Bernoulli percolation.
Given a set of vertices
A\subset\Lambda_{n}
, denote by
\mathfrak{S}(A)
the set of connected components (not to be confused with percolation clusters!) of
A
.
Below we denote by
\mathbb{P}^{\text{site}}_{p}
the law of Bernoulli site percolation of parameter
p
on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

Lemma 7.5
.

There exists
p_{0}<1
such that for every
\varepsilon>0
there exist
\ell=\ell(\varepsilon)\geq 1
and
c=c(\varepsilon)>0
such that

\mathbb{P}^{\textup{site}}_{p_{0}}\Bigg[\exists\,{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{m}):~|{\mathcal{C}}|\geq\tfrac{3}{4}|\Lambda_{m}|\text{ and }\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{S}\in\mathfrak{S}(\Lambda_{m}\setminus\mathcal{C})\\
|\mathcal{S}|\geq M\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{S}|\leq\varepsilon|\Lambda_{m}|\Bigg]\geq 1-e^{-cm^{d-1}}

(7.21)

for every
m\geq 1
.

Lemma
7.5
is a slight modification of the main result of
[
DP96
]
. The proof, very similar to that of
[
DP96
]
, is presented in the Appendix
A.3
.

Proof of Proposition
7.4
.

Fix
\beta>\beta_{c}
and
\delta>0
. We will prove the desired result by a renormalisation argument. Take
p_{0}<1
given by Lemma
7.5
. For
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
L\geq 1
, let
U_{x}(L)
denote the translation of the event
U(L)
from Theorem
1.4
by
x
. Consider the renormalised percolation model
\eta\in\{0,1\}^{\Lambda_{m}}
, where
m=\lfloor\frac{n-10L}{L}\rfloor
, given by

\eta_{x}\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}_{U_{Lx}(L)}.

Since
\inf_{\#}\Psi^{\#}_{\Lambda_{10L},\beta}[U(L)]\to 1
as
L\to\infty
, it follows from the main result of
[
LSS97
]
that there exists an
L=L(p_{0})\geq 1
sufficiently large such that, no matter the boundary condition
(\xi,\mathsf{b})
,
\eta
stochastically dominates Bernoulli percolation with parameter
p_{0}
. Let
\varepsilon>0
be a constant to be chosen later. By Lemma
7.5
, there exists
M=M(\varepsilon)\geq 1
and
c=c(\varepsilon)>0
such that

\Psi^{(\xi,\mathsf{b})}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\Bigg[\exists\,\mathcal{S}_{\rm max}\in\mathfrak{C}(\eta):~|\mathcal{S}_{\rm max}|\geq\tfrac{3}{4}|\Lambda_{m}|\text{ and }\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{m}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{S}\in\mathfrak{S}(\Lambda_{m}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\rm max})\\
|\mathcal{S}|\geq M\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{S}|\leq\varepsilon\Bigg]\geq 1-e^{-cm^{d-1}},

(7.22)

for every boundary condition
(\xi,\mathsf{b})
and for every
n
large enough.

We call
\mathcal{G}
the event in (
7.22
).
We claim that
\mathcal{G}
is contained in the complement of the event in (
7.20
) for
\varepsilon:=\frac{\min\{\delta,1\}}{2|\Lambda_{16L}|}
. This fact, combined with (
7.22
), implies the desired result. Let us prove this claim. The proof goes in two steps.

Because of the way the event
U(L)
is defined, the cluster
\mathcal{S}_{\rm max}
as in (
7.22
) induces an
\omega
cluster
\mathcal{C}_{0}
which intersects all boxes
\Lambda_{L}(Lx)
for
x\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm max}
. In particular if
\mathcal{G}
occurs,
\mathcal{C}_{0}
has size
|\mathcal{C}_{0}|\geq\frac{3}{4}|\Lambda_{m}|
. We first prove that
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is the largest
\omega
cluster, i.e.
\mathcal{C}_{0}=\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}
. To see this, let
N^{\prime}:=M|\Lambda_{16L}|
. Consider any other
\omega
-cluster
\mathcal{C}
such that
|\mathcal{C}\cap\Lambda_{n-11L}|\geq N^{\prime}
, and let
\mathcal{S}
be the set of vertices
x\in\Lambda_{m}
such that the annulus
\Lambda_{4L}(Lx)\setminus\Lambda_{2L}(Lx)
is crossed by
\mathcal{C}
. Note that

|\mathcal{C}\cap\Lambda_{n-11L}|\leq|\Lambda_{16L}||\mathcal{S}|,

(7.23)

hence
|\mathcal{S}|\geq M
. Moreover, we have

\mathcal{S}\subset\Lambda_{m}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\rm max}.

(7.24)

Thus,
\mathcal{S}
lies in a connected component of
\Lambda_{m}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\rm max}
, and in particular,
|\mathcal{S}|\leq\varepsilon|\Lambda_{m}|
. Hence, by our choice of
\varepsilon
,
|\mathcal{C}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|\Lambda_{m}|+|\Lambda_{n}\setminus\Lambda_{n-11L}|<|\mathcal{C}_{0}|
for every
n
large enough. This implies that
\mathcal{C}_{0}=\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}
.

Now, if
\mathcal{G}
occurs, we can use (
7.23
) and (
7.24
) to deduce that

\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}\setminus\{\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}\}\\
|\mathcal{C}\cap\Lambda_{n-11L}|\geq N^{\prime}\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{C}\cap\Lambda_{n-11L}|\leq\frac{|\Lambda_{16L}|}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{S}\in\mathfrak{S}(\Lambda_{m}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\rm max})\\
|\mathcal{S}|\geq M\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{S}|\leq\frac{\varepsilon|\Lambda_{16L}||\Lambda_{m}|}{|\Lambda_{n}|}.

(7.25)

Hence, for
N=2N^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}\setminus\{\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}\}\\
|\mathcal{C}|\geq N\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{C}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}\setminus\{\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}\}\\
|\mathcal{C}\cap\Lambda_{n-11L}|\leq N^{\prime}\\
|\mathcal{C}|\geq N\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{C}|+\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}\setminus\{\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}\}\\
|\mathcal{C}\cap\Lambda_{n-11L}|\geq N^{\prime}\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{C}\setminus\Lambda_{n-11L}|

(7.26)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}\setminus\{\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}\}\\
|\mathcal{C}\cap\Lambda_{n-11L}|\geq N^{\prime}\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{C}\cap\Lambda_{n-11L}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\Lambda_{n}\setminus\Lambda_{n-11L-N^{\prime}}|}{|\Lambda_{n}|}+\frac{\varepsilon|\Lambda_{16L}||\Lambda_{m}|}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\leq\frac{\delta}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon|\Lambda_{16L}||\Lambda_{m}|}{|\Lambda_{n}|}<\delta

for every
n
large enough. This proves the claim that the event in (
7.22
) is contained in the complement of the event in (
7.20
). The desired result follows.
∎

We are now in a position to prove the main result of this section.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

We fix
\beta>\beta_{c}
and drop it from the notations. Let
\delta>0
.

Proof of the upper bound in (
1.10
).

Let
M:=M_{0}(\delta/8)
be given by Lemma
7.1
. Let
N:=N_{0}(\delta/(8M))
be given by Proposition
7.4
. Applying these two results, and also Lemma
7.2
to
K=N
and
\tfrac{\delta}{4}
, we obtain
c_{1}>0
such that, for every
n
large enough,

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}[B_{1}\cup B_{2}]\leq e^{-c_{1}n^{d}},\qquad\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}[B_{3}]\leq e^{-c_{1}n^{d-1}},

(7.27)

where
B_{1}:=\left\{\tfrac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{a}_{x}\geq M}\geq\frac{\delta}{8}\right\}
,
B_{2}:=\left\{\tfrac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{|\mathcal{C}_{x}|\geq N}\leq m^{*}-\frac{\delta}{4}\right\}
, and
B_{3}:=\left\{\tfrac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}\setminus\{\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}\}:|\mathcal{C}|\geq N}|\mathcal{C}|\geq\tfrac{\delta}{8M}\right\}
. First, we remark that

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}[B_{4}]\leq\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}[B_{1}\cup B_{3}],

(7.28)

where
B_{4}:=\left\{\tfrac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}\setminus\{\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}\}:|\mathcal{C}|\geq N}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{C}}\mathsf{a}_{x}\geq\tfrac{\delta}{4}\right\}
. Combined with (
7.27
), this implies that for every
n
large enough,

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}[B_{4}]\leq 2e^{-c_{1}n^{d-1}}.

(7.29)

Now, remark that,

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}\left[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\notin\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}}\varphi_{x}\notin(-\tfrac{\delta}{2},\tfrac{\delta}{2})\right]\leq\Psi_{\Lambda_{n}}^{0}[B_{1}\cup B_{4}]+2\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}[C_{1}],

(7.30)

where

C_{1}:=\left\{\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}\setminus\{\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}\}\\
|\mathcal{C}|\leq N\end{subarray}}\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{C}}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{a}_{x}\leq M}\geq\frac{\delta}{8}\right\},

(7.31)

and we recall that
\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}
denotes the sign of
\mathcal{C}
.
Using the Edwards–Sokal coupling, we get that— conditionally on
\mathsf{a}
— the random variables

\mathbbm{1}_{|\mathcal{C}|\leq N}\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{C}}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{a}_{x}\leq M},\qquad\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}\setminus\{\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}\}

(7.32)

are centred, independent, and bounded (by
N\cdot M
). Classical large deviations estimates (see
[
Dur19
]
) then imply the existence of
c_{2}>0
such that for every
n
large enough,

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}[C_{1}]\leq e^{-c_{2}n^{d}}.

(7.33)

Combining (
7.27
), (
7.29
), (
7.30
), and (
7.33
), we obtain
c_{3}>0
such that for all
n
large enough,

\nu_{\Lambda_{n}}\left[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\varphi_{x}\in[-m^{*}+\delta,m^{*}-\delta]\right]\leq e^{-c_{3}n^{d-1}}+\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}\left[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}}\leq m^{*}-\frac{\delta}{2}\right].

(7.34)

Now, writing
\mathbbm{1}_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}}=\mathbbm{1}_{|\mathcal{C}_{x}|\geq N}-\mathbbm{1}_{|\mathcal{C}_{x}|\geq N,\>\mathcal{C}_{x}\neq\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}}
, observe that

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}\left[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}}\leq m^{*}-\frac{\delta}{2}\right]\leq\Psi_{\Lambda_{n}}^{0}[B_{2}\cup B_{4}].

(7.35)

Combining (
7.34
) and (
7.35
), and using again (
7.27
) and (
7.29
), we obtain that there exists
c_{4}>0
such that for all
n
large enough

\nu_{\Lambda_{n}}\left[\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\varphi_{x}\in[-m^{*}+\delta,m^{*}-\delta]\right]\leq e^{-c_{4}n^{d-1}}.

(7.36)

Proof of the lower bound in (
1.10
).

Let
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\delta)>0
be a small constant to be defined. We partition
\Lambda_{n}
into boxes of the form
\Lambda_{\varepsilon n}((\varepsilon n+1)x)\cap\Lambda_{n}
, where
x\in\Lambda_{m}
,
m=\lfloor\frac{n}{\varepsilon n+1}\rfloor
. Let
S
be the set of all the edges in
\Lambda_{n}
that connect neighbouring boxes
\Lambda_{\varepsilon n}((\varepsilon n+1)y)
and
\Lambda_{\varepsilon n}((\varepsilon n+1)x)
. Since
|S|\leq m^{d}\cdot 2d(2\varepsilon n)^{d-1}
, by the FKG inequality,

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}[\omega_{e}=0,\;\forall\;e\in S]\geq e^{-Cn^{d-1}}

(7.37)

for some constant
C=C(\varepsilon)>0
. Furthermore, by Proposition
2.6
, there exists a constant
R>0
, independent of
\varepsilon
, such that

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}\Big[\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{\varepsilon n}((\varepsilon n+1)x)}\mathsf{a}_{y}\leq R|\Lambda_{\varepsilon n}|,\;\forall x\in\Lambda_{m}\Big]\geq 1-|\Lambda_{m}|e^{-|\Lambda_{\varepsilon n}|}.

(7.38)

A union bound gives that for every
n
large enough

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}\Big[\omega_{e}=0,\,\forall\;e\in S,\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{\varepsilon n}((\varepsilon n+1)x)}\mathsf{a}_{y}\leq R|\Lambda_{\varepsilon n}|,\;\forall x\in\Lambda_{m}\Big]\geq e^{-2Cn^{d-1}}.

(7.39)

On the latter event, we use the Edwards–Sokal coupling to assign to each cluster independent
\pm 1
spins. Conditionally on such a pair
(\omega,\mathsf{a})
, the expectation of
\sum_{z\in\Lambda_{n}}\varphi_{z}
is equal to
0
. Furthermore, since on the event
\{\omega_{e}=0,\,\forall\;e\in S\}
spins in different
\varepsilon n
-boxes are independent, the (conditional) variance of
\sum_{z\in\Lambda_{n}}\varphi_{z}
is at most

\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{m}}\sum_{u,v\in\Lambda_{(\varepsilon n+1)x}}\mathsf{a}_{u}\mathsf{a}_{v}\leq m^{d}R^{2}|\Lambda_{\varepsilon n}|^{2}.

(7.40)

This implies that conditionally on such a pair
(\omega,\mathsf{a})
, with probability
1/2
we have that
|\sum_{z\in\Lambda_{n}}\varphi_{z}|\leq\sqrt{2m^{d}R^{2}|\Lambda_{\varepsilon n}|^{2}}
. Now, we choose
\varepsilon>0
to be small enough so that we have
2m^{d}R^{2}|\Lambda_{\varepsilon n}|^{2}\leq(m^{*}(\beta)-\delta)^{2}|\Lambda_{n}|^{2}
. The desired result follows by combining the above inequalities.
∎

We now prove the volume order large deviations mentioned in Remark
1.2
.

Proof of
(
1.11
)
.

We keep the notations of the preceding proof. For the lower bound, we note that for each
x\in\Lambda_{n}
, the probability that
\varphi_{x}\leq m^{*}(\beta)+\delta
stays bounded away from
0
. The FKG inequality then gives the desired lower bound.

For the upper bound, let
K_{1}=K_{1}(\delta/2)
be given by Lemma
7.3
. Using this result, there exists
c_{1}>0
such that for every
n
large enough,

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}\left[B_{5}\right]\leq e^{-c_{1}n^{d}},

(7.41)

where
B_{5}:=\left\{\tfrac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{|\mathcal{C}_{x}|\geq K_{1}}\geq m^{*}+\frac{\delta}{2}\right\}
. Notice that

\nu_{\Lambda_{n}}\Big[m_{\Lambda_{n}}\notin[-m^{*}-\delta,m^{*}+\delta]\Big]\leq\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}\left[B_{1}\cup B_{5}\right]+\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}[C_{2}],

(7.42)

where

C_{2}:=\left\{\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{n}|}\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}:|\mathcal{C}|\leq K_{1}}\text{sgn}(\mathcal{C})\sum_{x\in\mathcal{C}}\mathsf{a}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{a}_{x}\leq M}\geq\frac{\delta}{4}\right\}.

(7.43)

Using again a classical large deviations estimate, we argue the existence of
c_{2}>0
such that for every
n
large enough

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{n}}[C_{2}]\leq e^{-c_{2}n^{d}}.

(7.44)

Plugging (
7.41
), (
7.44
), and (
7.27
) in (
7.42
) yields the existence of
c_{3}>0
such that for every
n
large enough,

\nu_{\Lambda_{n}}\Big[m_{\Lambda_{n}}\notin[-m^{*}-\delta,m^{*}+\delta]\Big]\leq e^{-c_{3}n^{d}}.

(7.45)

∎

8
Spectral gap decay

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.3
as a consequence of Theorem
1.1
. We begin by introducing the necessary definitions before proceeding to the proof.

8.1
Definition of dynamics

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be finite and let
\beta>0
. A dynamical
\varphi^{4}
model on
\Lambda
at inverse temperature
\beta
is a continuous-time Markov process evolving on the state space
\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
with unique invariant measure
\nu_{\Lambda,\beta}
. As stated in the introduction, we focus on Langevin and heat-bath dynamics.

In order to define both dynamics precisely, let us recall some basic facts about Feller processes. Given a Markov process
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, we consider the semigroup
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
which acts on bounded measurable functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
via

P_{t}f(\varphi):=\mathbf{E}_{\varphi}[f(X_{t})],

(8.1)

where
\mathbf{E}_{\varphi}
denotes expectation with respect to the path measure
\mathbf{P}_{\varphi}
under which the process is conditioned to satisfy
X_{0}=\varphi
almost surely. Feller processes correspond to processes whose semigroups have a further regularity property when restricting its action to
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda})
, the space of continuous functions vanishing at infinity. Namely, for every
t\geq 0
,
P_{t}:C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda})\rightarrow C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda})
. It is well known that Feller processes are characterised by a generator, i.e.
P_{t}=e^{t\mathcal{L}}
for some negative-definite linear operator
\mathcal{L}
acting on a dense domain of
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda})
. Furthermore, we only consider examples where
\mathcal{L}
is (or rather, extends to) a self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\nu_{\Lambda,\beta})
. This means that the process is reversible.

We now turn to the precise definition of Langevin and heat-bath dynamics.

8.1.1
Langevin dynamics

We begin by describing the Langevin dynamics. Recall that the graph Laplacian
\Delta_{\Lambda}:\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
is the linear map defined by

(\Delta_{\Lambda}\varphi)_{x}=\sum_{y\sim x}(\varphi_{y}-\varphi_{x}),\qquad\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda},\,\forall x\in\Lambda.

(8.2)

Let us write

e^{-\beta H_{\Lambda}(\varphi)}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}{\rm d}\rho_{g,a}(\varphi_{x})=e^{-U_{\Lambda,\beta}(\varphi)-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{xy\in E}(\varphi_{x}-\varphi_{y})^{2}}\prod_{x\in\Lambda}{\rm d}\varphi_{x},

(8.3)

where the potential
U_{\Lambda,\beta}
is given by

U_{\Lambda,\beta}(\varphi)=\sum_{x\in V}\left(g\varphi_{x}^{4}+(a-\beta{\rm deg}_{\Lambda}(x)/2)\varphi_{x}^{2}\right),

(8.4)

and
{\rm deg}_{\Lambda}(x)
is the degree of
x
in
\Lambda
.

The Langevin dynamics
(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\rm LA})_{t\geq 0}
started from
\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
is the solution of a system of stochastic differential equations:

{\rm d}\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\rm LA}=\left(\beta\Delta_{\Lambda}\mathbf{X}^{\rm LA}_{t}-\nabla U_{\Lambda,\beta}(\mathbf{X}^{\rm LA}_{t})\right){\rm d}t+\sqrt{2}{\rm d}B_{t}^{\Lambda},\qquad\mathbf{X}^{\rm LA}_{0}=\varphi,

(8.5)

where
\nabla
is the gradient on
\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
, and
(B_{t}^{\Lambda})_{t\geq 0}
is a vector of i.i.d. Brownian motions started from
0
at each lattice point in
\Lambda
. It is classical that
(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\rm LA})_{t\geq 0}
defines a continuous-time Markov process with invariant measure
\nu_{\Lambda,\beta}
and with corresponding generator acting on smooth functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
via

\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta}^{\rm LA}f(\varphi)=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla U_{\Lambda,\beta}(\varphi)\cdot\nabla f(\varphi)+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\Lambda}f(\varphi).

(8.6)

We refer to
[
RT96
]
for further details.

8.1.2
Heat-bath dynamics

We now turn our attention to defining heat-bath dynamics, which is a general state-space Markov chain (see
[
MT12
]
). We consider a family of
transition rates

\{p_{\Lambda,\beta}(\varphi,x;s):\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda},x\in\Lambda,s\in\mathbb{R}\}
. The transition rate
p_{\Lambda,\beta}(\varphi,x;s)
is the infinitesimal rate at which
\varphi
jumps to
\varphi^{(x,s)}
, which is the configuration obtained from
\varphi
by replacing its value at
x
by
s
. Heat-bath dynamics corresponds to defining the transition rates according to the conditional
\varphi^{4}
measure on the singleton
\{x\}
with boundary conditions given by
\varphi|_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}}
. Let us give a precise definition: for every
\varphi
,
x
, and
s
,

p_{\Lambda,\beta}(\varphi,x;s):=\nu_{\Lambda,\beta}(\varphi_{x}=s\mid\varphi_{\Lambda\setminus\{x\}})=\frac{e^{-gs^{4}-as^{2}+\beta s\sum_{y\sim x}\varphi_{y}}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-gt^{4}-at^{2}+\beta t\sum_{y\sim x}\varphi_{y}}{\rm d}t}.

(8.7)

We build a Markov process
(\mathbf{X}^{\rm HB}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
associated to these jump rates by a classical construction. Let
\mathcal{P}
be a homogeneous Poisson point process of rate 1 on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\Lambda
. Let us consider a sample of
\mathcal{P}
, i.e. a sequence of random space-time points
\mathcal{T}:=((t_{i},x_{i})_{i\geq 1}:0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots<t_{n}\leq\dots,\,x_{i}\in\Lambda\}
. Since
\mathcal{P}
has no atoms, the strict monotonicity of the times occurs almost surely. Starting from some initial configuration
\varphi^{0}
, or initial law
\tilde{\nu}
on
\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
, we then sequentially update the process
(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{\rm HB})_{t\geq 0}
conditionally on
\mathcal{P}
at the space-time points
\mathcal{T}
, where each time the spin configuration is resampled according to the transition rates defined above.

The above construction yields a Markov process with invariant measure
\nu_{\Lambda,\beta}
and generator
\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\rm HB}
whose action for every
f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda})
is given by

\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda,\beta}^{\rm HB}f(\varphi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{x\in\Lambda}p_{\Lambda,\beta}(\varphi,x;s)(f(\varphi^{(x,s)})-f(\varphi)){\rm d}s,\qquad\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}.

(8.8)

Remark 8.1
.

We restrict to heat-bath dynamics for convenience. We expect our results to hold for a larger class of Glauber dynamics jump rates, but we do not pursue this direction further.

8.2
Spectral gap and proof of Theorem
1.3

We first recall some basic facts about spectral gaps in our context. As above, let
\mathcal{L}
be the generator of a Feller process that is reversible with respect to/self-adjoint in (a dense domain) of
L^{2}(\nu_{\Lambda,\beta})
. In the cases we consider,
\mathcal{L}
is a negative-definite linear operator and hence the spectrum is contained in
(-\infty,0]
. Furthermore, constant functions are eigenvectors of eigenvalue
0
. The spectral gap
\lambda(\mathcal{L})
is the largest
\lambda\geq 0
such that the spectrum of
\mathcal{L}
on the subspace orthogonal to constant functions is contained in
(-\infty,-\lambda]
. We can give an equivalent formulation in terms of the Dirichlet form
\mathcal{E}
defined by:

\mathcal{E}(f):=\langle-\mathcal{L}f\cdot f\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta},

(8.9)

where
f
is taken in the domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E})
of
\mathcal{E}
(appropriately defined). The spectral gap is characterised by the best constant in the Poincaré inequality, that is

\lambda(\mathcal{L})=\inf_{f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}):{\rm Var}_{\Lambda,\beta}(f)\neq 0}\frac{\mathcal{E}(f)}{{\rm Var}_{\Lambda,\beta}(f)}.

(8.10)

Remark 8.2
.

Recall the semigroup
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. For every
t\geq 0
,
P_{t}=e^{t\mathcal{L}}
. By this observation and standard results from spectral theory,
\lambda(\mathcal{L})>0
implies an exponential relaxation of variances. For every
f
in the domain of
\mathcal{L}
and every
t\geq 0
,

{\rm Var}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left(\mathbf{E}_{\varphi}[f(X_{t})]\right)\leq e^{-2\lambda(\mathcal{L})t}{\rm Var}_{\Lambda,\beta}(f).

(8.11)

It turns out that the two dynamics we consider are known to have strictly positive spectral gaps. For Langevin dynamics, this follows from functional inequalities developed in
[
Led01
]
(which apply since the potential
U_{\Lambda,\beta}
is strictly convex at infinity).

In the case of heat-bath dynamics, it is known that strict positivity of spectral gaps is equivalent to a condition known as
geometric ergodicity

[
RT01
]
. In order to state this, let us observe that, for every
\varphi
and every
t\geq 0
, the map
A\mapsto\mathbf{E}_{\varphi}[\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X_{t})]
defines a probability measure. We call this measure
P_{t}^{\varphi}
. Geometric ergodicity asserts that there exists
c>0
such that, almost surely for every
\varphi\sim\nu_{\Lambda,\beta}
, there exists
C(\varphi)>0
, such that

\|P_{t}^{\varphi}-\nu_{\Lambda,\beta}\|_{{\rm TV}}\leq C(\varphi)e^{-ct},

(8.12)

where
\|\cdot\|_{{\rm TV}}
is the total variation distance of these measures. Let us stress that, unlike the case of finite state-space chains, we cannot take
C(\varphi)
uniform in
\varphi
. The geometric ergodicity of heat-bath dynamics was established, as a special case of geometric ergodicity of Metropolis–Hastings chains (see the definition of Gibbs sampler in
[
RR04
]
), in
[
RT96b
]
.

Remark 8.3
.

For full disclosure, the aforementioned geometric ergodicity results are proven for the discrete-time analogues of heat-bath chains. As with finite state-space Markov chains, one can deduce ergodicity results for continuous-time chains from the results for discrete-time chains.

We now prove the surface order exponential decay of spectral gaps for
\varphi^{4}
dynamics in the supercritical regime. We only do the case of heat-bath dynamics since the proof for Langevin dynamics is similar and simpler (see for example the spectral gap estimates in
[
CGW22
]
). Additionally, we drop the superscript “HB” in all the notations.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

We let
d\geq 2
and
\beta>\beta_{c}
. We also take
\Lambda=\Lambda_{n}
for some
n
. Let
m\in(0,m^{*}(\beta)-3\delta)
and let
\chi_{m}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[-1,1]
be a smooth, increasing, and odd function satisfying:
\chi_{m}(a)\equiv-1
for
a\leq-m
and
\chi_{m}(a)\equiv 1
for
a\geq m
. Let us write

f(\varphi):=\chi_{m}(m_{\Lambda_{n}}(\varphi)).

(8.13)

By symmetry, one has that
\langle f\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}=0
. Moreover,

{\rm Var}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(f)=\langle f^{2}\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\geq 2\nu_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}[m_{\Lambda_{n}}\geq m]\geq 2\nu_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}[m_{\Lambda_{n}}\geq m^{*}(\beta)-3\delta].

(8.14)

Recall the definition of the Dirichlet form. In the case of heat-bath dynamics, it is explicitly given by:

\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(f)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\Big\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(\varphi,x;s)|f(\varphi)-f(\varphi^{(x,s)})|^{2}{\rm d}s\Big\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}.

(8.15)

Now, observe that

|f(\varphi)-f(\varphi^{(x,s)})|\leq|\chi_{m}^{\prime}|_{\infty}\frac{|\varphi_{x}|+|s|}{n^{d}}\mathbbm{1}\{m_{\Lambda_{n}}\in[-m-(|\varphi_{x}|+|s|)n^{-d},m+(|\varphi_{x}|+|s|)n^{-d}]\}.

(8.16)

We need to control the contribution of the term
|\varphi_{x}|+|s|
. In order to do so, let us define the events

\mathcal{G}_{1}(x):=\{|\varphi_{x}|\leq\delta n^{d}\},\qquad\mathcal{G}_{2}:=\{|s|\leq\delta n^{d}\}.

(8.17)

Note that by the Markov property and regularity, there exists
c_{1}>0
such that, for every
n
large enough,

\Big\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(\varphi,x;s)\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{G}_{2}^{c}\}{\rm d}s\Big\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}=\nu_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}[\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)^{c}]\leq\exp(-c_{1}n^{4d}).

(8.18)

Thus, since
|f|_{\infty}\leq 1
, there exists
c_{2}>0
such that, for every
n
large enough,

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\Big\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(\varphi,x;s)|f(\varphi)-f(\varphi^{(x,s)})|^{2}

\displaystyle(\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)^{c}\}+\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{G}_{2}^{c}\}){\rm d}s\Big\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}

(8.19)

\displaystyle\leq 8\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\nu_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}[\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)^{c}]

(8.20)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-c_{2}n^{4d}),

(8.21)

where we used the fact that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(\varphi,x;s){\rm d}s=1
and
\|f\|_{\infty}=1
in the second line, and (
8.18
) in the third line.
Let us define the event

\mathcal{E}=\{m_{\Lambda_{n}}\in(-m^{*}(\beta)+\delta,m^{*}(\beta)-\delta)\}.

(8.22)

Using Theorem
1.1
, there exists
c_{3}>0
such that for every
n
large enough,

\nu_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}[\mathcal{E}]\leq\exp(-c_{3}n^{d-1}).

(8.23)

Then, there exists
c_{4}>0
such that for every
n
large enough,

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\Big\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(\varphi,x;s)|f(\varphi)

\displaystyle-f(\varphi^{(x,s)})|^{2}(\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)\cap\mathcal{G}_{2}\}){\rm d}s\Big\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}

(8.24)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4|\chi_{m}^{\prime}|_{\infty}^{2}}{n^{2d}}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\Big\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(\varphi,x;s)(|\varphi_{x}|^{2}+|s|^{2})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}\}{\rm d}s\Big\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}

(8.25)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8|\chi_{m}^{\prime}|_{\infty}^{2}}{n^{2d}}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}}\nu_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}[|\varphi_{x}|^{4}]^{1/2}\nu_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}[\mathcal{E}]^{1/2}

(8.26)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-c_{4}n^{d-1}),

(8.27)

where in the second line we used (
8.16
) and the fact that
\{m_{\Lambda_{n}}\in[-m-(|\varphi_{x}|+|s|)n^{-d},m+(|\varphi_{x}|+|s|)n^{-d}]\}\cap\mathcal{G}_{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{2}\subset\mathcal{E}
, in the third line we used the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality (twice)— applied to the measure
\nu_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}({\rm d}\varphi)p_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(\varphi,x;s){\rm d}s
— to argue that

\displaystyle\Big\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(\varphi,x;s)|s|^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}\}{\rm d}s\Big\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(\varphi,x;s)|s|^{4}\mathrm{d}s\Big\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}^{1/2}\nu_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}[\mathcal{E}]^{1/2}

(8.28)

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi_{x}^{4}\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}^{1/2}\nu_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}[\mathcal{E}]^{1/2},

(8.29)

and that

\Big\langle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(\varphi,x;s)|\varphi_{x}|^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}\}{\rm d}s\Big\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}\leq\langle\varphi_{x}^{4}\rangle_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}^{1/2}\nu_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}[\mathcal{E}]^{1/2},

(8.30)

and in the fourth line we used (
8.23
).

Combining (
8.15
), (
8.16
), (
8.21
), and (
8.27
) yields, for every
n
large enough

\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(f)\leq\exp(-c_{5}n^{d-1}).

(8.31)

Moreover, (
8.14
) and (
8.23
) imply that, for every
n
large enough,

\textup{Var}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(f)\geq\frac{1}{2}.

(8.32)

Putting the two last displayed equations together yields, for every
n
large enough,

\lambda(\Lambda_{n})\leq\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(f)}{\textup{Var}_{\Lambda_{n},\beta}(f)}\leq 2\exp(-c_{5}n^{d-1}),

(8.33)

and concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Toolbox

A.1
Large degree deviations

In this section, we obtain a large deviations estimate for the degrees
\Delta{\mathbf{n}}(x)
of a random current
\mathbf{n}
. Recall that for
e=uv
,
\varphi_{e}
denotes the product
\varphi_{u}\varphi_{v}
, and by convention
\varphi_{\mathfrak{g}}\equiv 1
.

Lemma A.1
.

For every
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
o\in\Lambda
,
\mathsf{h}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{\Lambda}
and every set of edges
\mathcal{E}
in
\Lambda[\mathsf{h}]
, we have

\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathsf{h}}\mathbf{E}_{\Lambda[\mathsf{h}],\beta}^{o\mathfrak{g}}\Big[2^{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbf{n}_{e}}\Big]=\langle\varphi_{o}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\exp(J_{e}\varphi_{e})\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathsf{h}}

(A.1)

and

\mathbf{E}_{\Lambda[\mathsf{h}],\beta}^{\emptyset}\Big[2^{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbf{n}_{e}}\Big]=\langle\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\exp(J_{e}\varphi_{e})\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathsf{h}},

(A.2)

where
J_{e}:=\beta\mathbbm{1}_{e\in E(\Lambda)}+\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\beta\mathsf{h}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{e=x\mathfrak{g}}
.
In particular, if the endpoints of
\mathcal{E}
are all contained in
\Delta\cup\{\mathfrak{g}\}
, for a set
\Delta\subset\Lambda
satisfying the stochastic domination (
2.10
) from Proposition
2.6
with some
C\in(0,\infty)
, then the right hand sides of both (
A.1
) and (
A.2
) are smaller than
e^{C^{\prime}|\mathcal{E}|}
for some constant
C^{\prime}\in(0,\infty)
depending only on
C
.

Proof.

We only prove (
A.1
). The proof of (
A.2
) is similar and simpler. For ease of notation, we write
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}_{\Lambda[\mathsf{h}],\beta}^{o\mathfrak{g}}
.
We use the binomial theorem to write

\mathbf{E}\left[2^{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbf{n}_{e}}\right]=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\sum_{k_{e}\geq 0}\mathbf{E}\left[\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\binom{\mathbf{n}_{e}}{k_{e}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbf{n}_{e}\geq k_{e}}\right].

(A.3)

To express the latter, given a current
\mathbf{n}
that satisfies
\mathbf{n}_{e}\geq k_{e}
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
, let
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}
be the current defined by
\tilde{\mathbf{n}}_{f}:=\mathbf{n}_{f}-k_{f}\mathbbm{1}\{f\in\mathcal{E}\}
. With this definition in hands, we can write

w^{o\mathfrak{g}}_{\beta,\mathsf{h}}(\mathbf{n})\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\binom{\mathbf{n}_{e}}{k_{e}}=w^{A}_{\beta,\mathsf{h}}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}\cap E(\Lambda)}\frac{\beta^{k_{e}}}{k_{e}!}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Lambda\\
e=x\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{E}\end{subarray}}\frac{(\beta\mathsf{h}_{x})^{k_{e}}}{k_{e}!},

(A.4)

where
A\in\mathcal{M}(\Lambda^{\mathfrak{g}})
is defined by

\displaystyle A_{x}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathcal{E}\\
\>e\ni x\end{subarray}}k_{e}+\mathbbm{1}_{x=o},\quad x\in\Lambda,

(A.5)

\displaystyle A_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathcal{E}\\
e\ni\mathfrak{g}\end{subarray}}k_{e}+1\text{ mod }2.

(A.6)

Moreover,
\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\partial A
.
Using Lemma
2.11
we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\binom{\mathbf{n}_{e}}{k_{e}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbf{n}_{e}\geq k_{e}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathsf{h}}}\frac{\sum_{\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\partial A}w^{A}_{\beta,\mathsf{h}}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})}{\sum_{\partial\tilde{\mathbf{n}}=\emptyset}w_{\beta,\mathsf{h}}(\tilde{\mathbf{n}})}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}\cap E(\Lambda)}\frac{\beta^{k_{e}}}{k_{e}!}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Lambda\\
e=x\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{E}\end{subarray}}\frac{(\beta\mathsf{h}_{x})^{k_{e}}}{k_{e}!}

(A.7)

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\varphi_{x}^{A_{x}}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathsf{h}}}{\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathsf{h}}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}\cap E(\Lambda)}\frac{\beta^{k_{e}}}{k_{e}!}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Lambda\\
e=x\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{E}\end{subarray}}\frac{(\beta\mathsf{h}_{x})^{k_{e}}}{k_{e}!}

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle\varphi_{o}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}\cap E(\Lambda)}(\beta\varphi_{e})^{k_{e}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\Lambda\\
e=x\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{E}\end{subarray}}(\beta\mathsf{h}_{x}\varphi_{x})^{k_{e}}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathsf{h}}}{\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathsf{h}}}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{1}{k_{e}!}.

We can now use (
A.3
) to deduce that

\mathbf{E}\left[2^{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbf{n}_{e}}\right]=\frac{\langle\varphi_{o}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\exp(J_{e}\varphi_{e})\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathsf{h}}}{\langle\varphi_{o}\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathsf{h}}}.

(A.8)

∎

A.2
Tanglings estimates

In this section we prove Proposition
2.18
.
We recall from
[
GPPS22
]
that the measure
\rho^{2k}
(resp.
\rho^{2k_{1},2k_{2}}
) is constructed from taking a weak limit of the single (resp. double) random current measure associated with a near-critical Ising model on the complete graph
K_{n}
. We refer to
[
GPPS22
,
KPP24
]
for more details. In order to prove the desired result, we will use some properties of this random current expansion established in
[
KPP24
]
. We denote the random current measure on
K_{n}
with source set
S
by
P^{S}_{K_{n}}
.

Proof of Proposition
2.18
.

Let
S_{1}=\{1,2,\ldots,2k_{1}\}
and
S_{2}=\{2k_{1}+1,2k_{1}+2,\ldots,2k_{1}+2k_{2}\}
. Let
\mathbf{n}_{1}
and
\mathbf{n}_{2}
be random currents on
K_{n}
with source sets
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
, respectively. Let
\Pi_{i}(\mathbf{n}_{i})
be the random partition of
S_{i}
induced by the current
\mathbf{n}_{i}
, where
x,y
are in the same partition class if and only if
x\longleftrightarrow y
in
\mathbf{n}_{i}
. Define similarly
\Pi(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})
to be the random partition of
S_{1}\cup S_{2}
induced by the current
\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}
. We will show that for every even partition
P_{1}
of
S_{1}
and every even partition
P_{2}
of
S_{2}
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that

P^{S_{1},S_{2}}_{K_{n}}[\Pi(\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2})=\{S_{1}\cup S_{2}\}\mid\Pi_{1}(\mathbf{n}_{1})=P_{1},\Pi_{2}(\mathbf{n}_{2})=P_{2}]\geq\varepsilon

(A.9)

for every
n
large enough. The desired result will then follow by taking the limit as
n
tends to infinity.

Indeed, by the main result of
[
KPP24
]
, there exist
\delta>0
and
c>0
such that

P^{S_{1}}_{K_{n}}[\Pi_{1}(\mathbf{n}_{1})=P_{1},|C_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(i)|\geq c\sqrt{n}\;\forall i\in S_{1}]\geq\delta

(A.10)

and

P^{S_{2}}_{K_{n}}[\Pi_{2}(\mathbf{n}_{2})=P_{2},\sqrt{n}/c\geq|C_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(i)|\geq c\sqrt{n}\;\forall i\in S_{2}]\geq\delta

(A.11)

for every
n
large enough, where
C_{\mathbf{n}_{i}}(x)
denotes the connected component of
x
induced by the current
\mathbf{n}_{i}
, and
|C_{\mathbf{n}_{i}}(x)|
denotes its cardinality (number of vertices). Our aim is to show that conditionally on the above events, there is positive probability that each cluster
C_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(i)
with
i\in S_{2}
intersects all clusters
C_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(j)
with
j\in S_{1}
. The desired result then follows readily.

To this end, it suffices to show that for every
i\in S_{2}
, conditionally on the clusters
C_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(1),\ldots,C_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(2k_{1})
and
C_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(2k_{1}+1),\ldots,C_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(i-1)
, the cluster
C_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(i)
has positive probability to intersect all clusters
C_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(1),\ldots,C_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(2k_{1})
. Note that under this conditioning,
C_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(i)\setminus S_{2}
is sampled uniformly at random from
K_{n}
without
C_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(2k_{1}+1),\ldots,C_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(i-1)
and
S_{2}
. For ease of notation we prove the above only for
i=2k_{1}+1
, and the general case follows similarly, up to changing the value of
n
.

Let
P_{1}=\{Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{j}\}
be an even partition of
S_{1}
, and on the event
\{\Pi(\mathbf{n}_{1})=P_{1}\}
, write
C_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(Q_{\ell})
for the cluster of
Q_{\ell}
in
\mathbf{n}_{1}
. Denote
A=A(B_{1},\ldots,B_{j},r)
the event
\{C_{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(Q_{\ell})=B_{\ell}
for every
\ell\in\{1,2,\ldots,j\},|C_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(2k_{1}+1)|=r\}
, where the sets
B_{\ell}
are possible realisations satisfying
|B_{\ell}|\geq c\sqrt{n}
, and where
\sqrt{n}/c\geq r\geq c\sqrt{n}
.
Note that under
P^{S_{2}}_{K_{n}}[\>\cdot\mid|C_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(2k_{1}+1)|=r]
, the vertices lying in
C_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(2k_{1}+1)\setminus\{2k_{1}+1,\ldots,2k_{1}+2k_{2}\}
are distributed uniformly at random among subsets of cardinality
r-2k_{2}
chosen from a set of cardinality
n-2k_{2}
. Furthermore, the probability that the set of these
r-2k_{2}
points intersects each
B_{\ell}
is increasing as a function of
r
and the size of each
B_{\ell}
. Thus, we may assume that each
B_{\ell}
has size
r_{0}:=\lfloor c\sqrt{n}\rfloor
, and
|C_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(2k_{1}+1)|=r_{0}
. By asking
C_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(2k_{1}+1)
to contain exactly one point from each
B_{\ell}
we obtain the lower bound

P^{S_{1},S_{2}}_{K_{n}}[2k_{1}+1\xleftrightarrow{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}\>}Q_{\ell},\>\forall\ell=1,2,\ldots,j\mid A]\geq\frac{(r_{0}-2k_{1}-2k_{2})^{j}\binom{n-m-2k_{2}}{r_{0}-j-2k_{2}}}{\binom{n-2k_{2}}{r_{0}-2k_{2}}},

(A.12)

where
m:=j(r_{0}-2k_{1}-2k_{2})
. Here the term
(r_{0}-2k_{1}-2k_{2})^{j}
is a lower bound for the number of ways to choose exactly one vertex from each
B_{\ell}
, and
\binom{n-m-2k_{2}}{r_{0}-j-2k_{2}}
is a lower bound for the number of ways to choose the remaining
r_{0}-j-2k_{2}
vertices.

For every
n
large enough we have

\displaystyle\frac{\binom{n-m-2k_{2}}{r_{0}-j-2k_{2}}}{\binom{n-2k_{2}}{r_{0}-j-2k_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(n-m-2k_{2})!(n-r_{0}+j)!}{(n-2k_{2})!(n-m-r_{0}+j)!}=\prod_{p=0}^{m-1}\frac{n-r_{0}+j-p}{n-2k_{2}-p}

(A.13)

\displaystyle=\prod_{p=0}^{m-1}\left(1-\frac{r_{0}-j-2k_{2}}{n-2k_{2}-p}\right)\geq\left(1-\frac{2r_{0}}{n}\right)^{m}=(1+o(1))e^{-2jc^{2}}.

Moreover, for some constant
c^{\prime}>0
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\binom{n-2k_{2}}{r_{0}-j-2k_{2}}}{\binom{n-2k_{2}}{r_{0}-2k_{2}}}=\frac{(r_{0}-2k_{2})!(n-r_{0})!}{(r_{0}-j-2k_{2})!(n-r_{0}+j)!}=(1+o(1))\frac{r_{0}^{j}}{n^{j}}\geq\frac{c^{\prime}}{(r_{0}-2k_{1}-2k_{2})^{j}}.

(A.14)

This implies that

P^{S_{1},S_{2}}_{K_{n}}[2k_{1}+1\xleftrightarrow{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}\>}Q_{\ell},\;\forall\ell=1,2,\ldots,j\mid A]\geq(1+o(1))c^{\prime}e^{-2jc^{2}}.

(A.15)

The desired result follows.
∎

A.3
Large deviation for highly supercritical percolation

Here we prove Lemma
7.5
by adapting
[
DP96
]
. We start by introducing some notations and recalling basic facts. Given a graph
G=(V,E)
and a subset
S\subset V
, we define the inner and outer boundaries
\partial^{\rm in}_{G}S\coloneqq\{x\in S:~\exists\,y\in V\setminus S,\,\{x,y\}\in E\}
and
\partial^{\rm out}_{G}S\coloneqq\{y\in V\setminus S:~\exists\,x\in S,\,\{x,y\}\in E\}
. We define the notion of
*
-connectivity in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, where every pair of vertices
x,y
satisfying
|x-y|_{\infty}=1
are connected by an edge.
We will use the fact, proved in
[
DP96
, Lemma 2.1 (ii)]
, that for every connected set
C\subset\Lambda_{m}
and every
S\in\mathfrak{S}(\Lambda_{m}\setminus C)
, both
\partial^{\rm in}_{\Lambda_{m}}S
and
\partial^{\rm out}_{\Lambda_{m}}S
are
*
-connected.

Our main tool will be a local isoperimetric inequality for boxes of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
d\geq 2
, proved in
[
DP96
, Proposition 2.2]
: for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a constant
c=c(\varepsilon)>0
such that for every
m\geq 1
and every connected set
S\subset\Lambda_{m}
satisfying
|S|\leq(1-\varepsilon)|\Lambda_{m}|
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}|\Delta_{i}S|^{\frac{d}{d-1}}\geq c|S|,

(A.16)

where
\Delta S
can be either
\partial^{\rm in}_{\Lambda_{m}}S
or
\partial^{\rm out}_{\Lambda_{m}}S
and
(\Delta_{i}S)_{i=1}^{\ell}
are the
*
-connected components of
\Delta S
.

Proof of Lemma
7.5
.

Fix
m\geq 1
. Recall that
\mathfrak{C}=\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{m})
denotes the set of open clusters. We also denote by
\mathfrak{C}^{*}=\mathfrak{C}^{*}(\Lambda_{m})
to be the set of closed
*
-connected clusters.

Assume that
|\mathcal{C}|<\tfrac{3}{4}|\Lambda_{m}|
for every
\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}
. For convenience, we extend
\mathfrak{C}
to
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
by including each closed vertex as a singleton. For every
\mathcal{C}\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
, let
\Delta\mathcal{C}=\partial^{\rm out}_{\Lambda_{m}}\mathcal{C}
if
\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}
, and
\Delta\mathcal{C}=\{x\}
if
\mathcal{C}=\{x\}
is a closed singleton. In particular, the
*
-connected components
(\Delta_{i}\mathcal{C})_{i=1}^{\ell(\mathcal{C})}
of
\Delta\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{C}\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}
, are all fully closed and, by our assumption, satisfy the local isoperimetric inequality (
A.16
) with
c=c(1/4)
. Since each vertex can only appear in at most
2d+1
many components
\Delta_{i}\mathcal{C}
, one has
(2d+1)|\mathcal{C}^{*}|\geq\sum_{\Delta_{i}\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{C}^{*}}|\Delta_{i}\mathcal{C}|
. Taking both sides to the power
d/(d-1)
and using (
A.16
), we conclude that

|\Lambda_{m}|=\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}}|\mathcal{C}|\leq\tfrac{1}{c}\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell(\mathcal{C})}|\Delta_{i}\mathcal{C}|^{\frac{d}{d-1}}\leq\tfrac{1}{c^{\prime}}\sum_{\mathcal{C}^{*}\in\mathfrak{C}^{*}}|\mathcal{C}^{*}|^{\frac{d}{d-1}},

(A.17)

where
c^{\prime}=c/(2d+1)^{\frac{d}{d-1}}
.

Let us now assume that there exists a component
\mathcal{C}\in\mathfrak{C}
such that
|\mathcal{C}|\geq\tfrac{3}{4}|\Lambda_{m}|
, but that
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{S}\in\mathfrak{S}(\Lambda_{m}\setminus\mathcal{C})\\
|\mathcal{S}|\geq M\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{S}|>\varepsilon|\Lambda_{m}|
.
For each
\mathcal{S}\in\mathfrak{S}(\Lambda_{m}\setminus\mathcal{C})
, let
\Delta\mathcal{S}\coloneqq\partial_{\Lambda_{m}}^{\rm in}\mathcal{S}
and notice that
\Delta\mathcal{S}
is closed,
*
-connected and satisfies the local isoperimetric inequality (
A.16
). Using again that each vertex appears in at most
2d+1
many
\Delta\mathcal{S}
, we conclude that

\varepsilon|\Lambda_{m}|<\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{S}\in\mathfrak{S}(\Lambda_{m}\setminus\mathcal{C})\\
|\mathcal{S}|\geq M\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{S}|\leq\tfrac{1}{c}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{S}\in\mathfrak{S}(\Lambda_{m}\setminus\mathcal{C})\\
|\Delta\mathcal{S}|\geq(cM)^{(d-1)/d}\end{subarray}}|\Delta\mathcal{S}|^{\frac{d}{d-1}}\leq\tfrac{1}{c^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{C}^{*}\in\mathfrak{C}^{*}\\
|\mathcal{C}^{*}|\geq(cM)^{(d-1)/d}\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{C}^{*}|^{\frac{d}{d-1}}.

(A.18)

Combining the two last paragraphs, we have

1-\mathbb{P}^{\textup{site}}_{p_{0}}[\mathcal{E}]\leq\mathbb{P}^{\textup{site}}_{p_{0}}\Bigg[\sum_{\mathcal{C}^{*}\in\mathfrak{C}^{*}}|\mathcal{C}^{*}|^{\frac{d}{d-1}}\geq c^{\prime}|\Lambda_{m}|\Bigg]+\mathbb{P}^{\textup{site}}_{p_{0}}\Bigg[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{C}^{*}\in\mathfrak{C}^{*}\\
|\mathcal{C}^{*}|\geq(cM)^{{(d-1)}/{d}}\end{subarray}}|\mathcal{C}^{*}|^{\frac{d}{d-1}}\geq c^{\prime}\varepsilon|\Lambda_{m}|\Bigg],

(A.19)

where
\mathcal{E}
is the event in (
7.21
).
Under a certain probability measure
\mathbb{P}
, let (
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{x})_{x\in\Lambda_{m}}
be independent random variables, each distributed as the closed
*
-connected cluster of
x
under
\mathbb{P}^{\textup{site}}_{p_{0}}
.
As proved in
[
DP96
, Lemma 2.3]
, for every increasing function
\rho:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have that
\sum_{\mathcal{C}^{*}\in\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{m})}\rho(|\mathcal{C}^{*}|)
is stochastically dominated by
\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{m}}\rho(|\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{x}|)
.
Therefore,

1-\mathbb{P}^{\textup{site}}_{p_{0}}[\mathcal{E}]\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{m}}|\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{x}|^{\frac{d}{d-1}}\geq c^{\prime}|\Lambda_{m}|\Bigg]+\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{m}}|\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{x}|^{\frac{d}{d-1}}\mathbbm{1}\{|\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{x}|\geq(cM)^{\frac{d-1}{d}}\}\geq c^{\prime}\varepsilon|\Lambda_{m}|\Bigg].

(A.20)

It is standard (see e.g.
[
DP96
, Equation (3.8)]
) to prove that for
p_{0}<1
sufficiently close to
1
, the tail
\mathbb{P}[|\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{x}|\geq k]
of the random variable
|\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{x}|
decays exponentially fast to
0
in
k
. We can further choose
p_{0}<1
such that
\mathbb{E}[|\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{x}|]\leq c^{\prime}/2
, and then choose
M=M(\varepsilon)\geq 1
large enough such that
\mathbb{E}[|\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{x}|\mathbbm{1}\{|\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{*}_{x}|\geq(cM)^{\frac{d-1}{d}}\}]\leq c^{\prime}\varepsilon/2
. It then follows from standard large deviation estimates for i.i.d. random variables (see e.g.
[
Dur19
]
) that both probabilities in the right hand side of (
A.20
) are decay exponentially in
|\Lambda_{m}|^{\frac{d-1}{d}}\asymp m^{d-1}
, as we wanted to prove.
∎

Appendix B
The weak plus measure

In this section, we consider the question of how small can a boundary field
\mathsf{h}_{L}
be so that
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\mathsf{h}_{L}}\underset{L\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\langle\cdot\rangle^{+}_{\beta}
. We show that the convergence holds for boundary fields satisfying
\mathsf{h}_{L}\leq\mathfrak{p}_{\Lambda_{L}}
and
L^{d-1}\mathsf{h}_{L}\to\infty
, which was claimed in Remark
2.10
. We first need the following lemma.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be a finite set and let
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{\partial\Lambda}
such that
|\eta|\leq\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}_{\Lambda}
. There exists a coupling
(\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta},\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}},\varphi^{\eta})
,
\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}\sim\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathfrak{p}}
,
\varphi^{\eta}\sim\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\eta}
, such that
\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta}
-almost surely,
|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x}|\geq|\varphi^{\eta}_{x}|
and

\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x})-\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\eta}_{x})\mid|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}|,|\varphi^{\eta}|\right]\geq 0

(B.1)

for every
x\in\Lambda
.

Proof.

Using Lemma 2.13 in
[
GPPS22
]
and Strassen’s theorem
[
Str65
]
, we obtain a coupling
(\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta},\psi^{\mathfrak{p}},\psi^{\eta})
, where
\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}\sim\langle|\cdot|\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathfrak{p}}
and
\psi^{\eta}\sim\langle|\cdot|\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\eta}
, such that
\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta}
-almost surely,

\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x}\geq\psi^{\eta}_{x}\quad\text{for every }x\in\Lambda.

(B.2)

Enlarging our probability space, we can assume that in the same probability space, there is a family
I=\{\sigma^{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathsf{a})\sim\langle\cdot\rangle^{\textup{Ising},0}_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathfrak{p},\mathsf{a}},\sigma^{\eta}(\mathsf{a})\sim\langle\cdot\rangle^{\textup{Ising},0}_{\Lambda,\beta,\eta,\mathsf{a}}\mid\mathsf{a}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{\Lambda}\}
of independent Ising models that are also independent from
\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\psi^{\eta}
. Now let
\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}:=\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot\sigma^{\mathfrak{p}}(\psi^{\mathfrak{p}})
and
\varphi^{\eta}:=\psi^{\eta}\cdot\sigma^{\eta}(\psi^{\eta})
. It follows that
\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}\sim\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathfrak{p}}
,
\varphi^{\eta}\sim\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\eta}
, and
|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x}|\geq|\varphi^{\eta}_{x}|
for every
x\in\Lambda
.
Moreover, by the Ginibre
inequality for the Ising model,

\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}\left[\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{x})-\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\eta}_{x})\mid|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}|,|\varphi^{\eta}|\right]=\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle^{\textup{Ising},0}_{\Lambda,\beta,\mathfrak{p},\psi^{\mathfrak{p}}}-\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle^{\textup{Ising},0}_{\Lambda,\beta,\eta,\psi^{\eta}}\geq 0.

(B.3)

∎

We now prove the main result of this section.

Proposition B.2
.

Let
\beta>\beta_{c}
. Let
\eta_{L}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{\partial\Lambda_{L}}
be an external magnetic field such that
\eta_{L}\leq\mathfrak{p}_{\Lambda_{L}}
and
L^{d-1}\inf_{x\in\partial\Lambda}\eta_{L}(x)\to\infty
as
L\to\infty
. Then for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

\lim_{L\to\infty}\langle\varphi_{x}\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\eta_{L}}=\langle\varphi_{x}\rangle^{+}_{\beta}.

(B.4)

As a consequence, the sequence of measures
(\langle\cdot\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\eta_{L}})_{L\geq 1}
converges weakly towards
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\beta}^{+}
.

Proof.

Assuming the first part, the second part of the statement follows from the strategy described in Remark
3.2
. We therefore focus on proving (
B.4
). We do the computation for
x=0
as the general case follows by the same argument.

We claim that it suffices to show that

\lim_{L\to\infty}\langle\textup{sgn}(\varphi_{0})\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\eta_{L}}=\langle\textup{sgn}(\varphi_{0})\rangle^{+}_{\beta}.

(B.5)

Indeed, assume that (
B.5
) holds and recall the monotone coupling
(\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,\beta},\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}},\varphi^{\eta_{L}})
of Lemma
B.1
. Let
Y=\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{0})-\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\eta}_{0})
. Using the defining properties of the monotone coupling, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the fact that
0\leq Y\leq 2
we obtain that

\displaystyle\langle\varphi_{0}\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\mathfrak{p}_{L}}-\langle\varphi_{0}\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\eta_{L}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}[(|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}|-|\varphi^{\eta_{L}}_{0}|)\textup{sgn}(\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{0})]+\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}[|\varphi^{\eta_{L}}_{0}|Y]

(B.6)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}[|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}|-|\varphi^{\eta_{L}}_{0}|]+\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}[(\varphi^{\eta_{L}}_{0})^{2}]\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}[Y^{2}]}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}[|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}|-|\varphi^{\eta_{L}}_{0}|]+\sqrt{2\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}[(\varphi^{\eta_{L}}_{0})^{2}]\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}[Y]}.

Since
\langle|\varphi_{0}|\rangle_{\Lambda,\beta,\eta_{L}}\geq\langle|\varphi_{0}|\rangle^{0}_{\Lambda,\beta}
, and
\Psi^{0}=\Psi^{1}
by Proposition
4.13
, it follows that
\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}[|\varphi^{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}|-|\varphi^{\eta_{L}}_{0}|]
tends to
0
. Furthermore,
\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\beta}[Y]
tends to
0
by our assumption. We can thus conclude that

\lim_{L\to\infty}\langle\varphi_{0}\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\eta_{L}}=\langle\varphi_{0}\rangle^{+}_{\beta},

(B.7)

as desired.

We now proceed with the proof of (
B.5
).
By the Edwards–Sokal coupling and Proposition
4.13
, it suffices to prove that

\lim_{L\to\infty}\Psi_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\eta_{L}}^{0}[0\longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{g}]=\Psi^{0}_{\beta}[0\longleftrightarrow\infty].

(B.8)

We will prove this by using a renormalisation argument.
To this end, recall Theorem
1.4
. Let
\varepsilon>0
and consider some
s\in(0,1)
and
k\geq 1
to be chosen in terms of
\varepsilon
. Let
L\geq 1
be much larger than
k
, and consider boxes of the form
\Lambda_{k}(kx)
for
x\in\Lambda_{m}
, where
m=\lfloor\frac{L-10k}{k}\rfloor
. We define a site percolation configuration
\gamma
on
\Lambda_{m}
by letting
\gamma_{x}=1
if
U(k,kx)
happens and
\gamma_{x}=0
otherwise. As in the proof of Proposition
6.4
, there exists
k=k(s)
large enough, such that
\gamma
dominates a Bernoulli site percolation
\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda_{m},s}
of parameter
s
. We will choose
s
sufficiently close to
1
(which corresponds to choosing
k
large enough) below.

Let
P_{m}
denote the projection of
\Lambda_{m/2}
on a fixed side of
\partial\Lambda_{m}
. Let also
\mathcal{E}
denote the event that the number of vertices
x\in P_{m}
that are connected to
\Lambda_{m/2}
by a path of open vertices is at least
m^{d-1}/2^{d}
. We will use a Peierls-type argument to show that
\mathcal{E}
occurs with probability close to
1
under
\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda_{m},s}
. To this end, for
x\in\partial\Lambda_{m}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{x}
denote the connected component of
x
in
\Lambda_{m}
consisting of open vertices, and define
\mathcal{C}=\bigcup_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{m}}\mathcal{C}_{x}
. For
x\in P_{m}
, let
K_{x}
denote the column that contains
x
and intersects
\Lambda_{m/2}
. Note that when there is no path of open vertices connecting a vertex
x\in P_{m}
to
\Lambda_{m/2}
, there exists a vertex
y\in\partial^{\mathrm{ext}}\mathcal{C}
that lies in
K_{x}
. Indeed, let
z
be the last vertex of
K_{x}
that lies in
\mathcal{C}_{x}
, and let
y
be the vertex in
K_{x}
after
z
. Then
y
lies in
\partial^{\mathrm{ext}}\mathcal{C}
, as claimed. We can now deduce that

\mathbb{P}_{A_{m},s}[\mathcal{E}^{c}]\leq\mathbb{P}_{A_{m},s}\left[|\partial^{\mathrm{ext}}\mathcal{C}|\geq m^{d-1}/2^{d}\right],

(B.9)

where we used that
|P_{m}|-m^{d-1}/2^{d}\geq m^{d-1}/2^{d}
.

To bound the latter probability, let
\Delta\mathcal{C}=\partial^{\mathrm{ext}}\mathcal{C}\cup\partial^{\rm ext}\Lambda_{m}
. Note that
\Delta\mathcal{C}
is
*
-connected, since each
\partial^{\mathrm{ext}}\mathcal{C}_{x}
is
*
-connected. Since the number of
*
-connected subgraphs of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
k
vertices that contain
0
is at most
e^{Ck}
for some constant
C>0
, we can use a union bound to obtain that

\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda_{m},s}\left[|\partial^{\mathrm{ext}}\mathcal{C}|\geq m^{d-1}/2^{d}\right]\leq\sum_{i\geq m^{d-1}/2^{d}}\exp\left(C|\partial^{\rm ext}\Lambda_{m}|+Ci\right)(1-s)^{i}.

(B.10)

By choosing
s
to be close enough to
1
, we can ensure that the latter is at most
\varepsilon
, which implies that

\limsup_{L\to\infty}\Psi_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\eta_{L}}^{0}[\gamma\in\mathcal{E}^{c}]\leq\varepsilon.

(B.11)

Now, note that when the events
\mathcal{E}
and
\{0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{m}\}
happen under
\gamma
, and additionally,
\{0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{k}\}
and
U(L)
happen under
\omega
, then the event
\mathcal{B}=\{0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{L-10k},|\mathcal{C}_{0}\cap\partial\Lambda_{L-10k}|\geq cL^{d-1}\}
happens under
\omega
, for some
c>0
. By further increasing the value of
s
, we can assume that

\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda_{m},s}[0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{m}]\geq 1-\varepsilon
. Choosing
L
to be large enough so that
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\eta_{L}}[U(L)]\geq 1-\varepsilon
and
\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\eta_{L}}[0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{k}]\geq\Psi^{0}_{\beta}[0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{k}]-\varepsilon
, we obtain that

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\eta_{L}}[\mathcal{B}]\geq\Psi^{0}_{\beta}[0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{k}]-4\varepsilon\geq\Psi^{0}_{\beta}[0\longleftrightarrow\infty]-4\varepsilon.

(B.12)

It remains to show that conditionally on
\mathcal{B}
,
0
is connected to
\mathfrak{g}
with high probability.
Indeed, if some
x\in\Lambda_{L-10k}
is connected to
0
, when we fully open
E(\Lambda_{10k}(x))
and we also open an edge of the form
u\mathfrak{g}
for some
u\in\Lambda_{10k}(x)
,
0
is connected to
\mathfrak{g}
. Now note that

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{10k}(x),\beta,\eta_{L}}[\omega|_{E(\Lambda_{10k}(x))\cup\{u\mathfrak{g}\}}=1]\geq\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{10k}(x),\beta}[\omega|_{E(\Lambda_{10k}(x))}=1]\Psi^{0}_{\{u\},\beta,\eta_{L}}[\omega_{u\mathfrak{g}}=1]\geq r\eta_{L},

(B.13)

for some
r>0
by the FKG inequality and monotonicity in the volume.
It follows that the family of events
\{\omega|_{E(\Lambda_{10k}(x))\cup\{u\mathfrak{g}\}}=1\}
for
x\in\mathcal{C}_{0}\cap\partial\Lambda_{L-10k}
at distance at least
20k
apart from each other stochastically dominates a sequence of Bernoulli random variables of parameter
r\eta_{L}>0
. Hence, conditionally on
\mathcal{B}
,
\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{0}\cap\partial\Lambda_{L-10k}}\{\omega|_{E(\Lambda_{10k}(x))\cup\{u\mathfrak{g}\}}=1\}
happens with high probability, where here we use that
L^{d-1}\eta_{L}\to\infty
.
Thus, for every
L
large enough we have

\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\eta_{L}}[0\longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{g}\mid\mathcal{B}]\geq 1-\varepsilon.

(B.14)

It follows that

\liminf_{L\to\infty}\Psi^{0}_{\Lambda_{L},\beta,\eta_{L}}[0\longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{g}]\geq(1-\varepsilon)(\Psi^{0}_{\beta}[0\longleftrightarrow\infty]-4\varepsilon).

(B.15)

Since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, we can conclude that
(
B.8
) holds, as desired.
∎
